# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-19

## Multimodal Learning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[CIIR - Class-Indexed Interface Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23151v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal learning commonly assumes paired or synchronized observations, yet independently collected datasets rarely provide such correspondence. We introduce CIIR, a modular encoder--decoder framework that connects heterogeneous datasets through a class-indexed address space while preserving each dataset's native input format and output module. A known local index selects address $k$, and an input-dependent 128-dimensional address representation Z_k(x) is written to that address. To demonstrate CIIR, we learn this address space from handwritten digits, clothing images, spoken-digit spectrograms, and hand signs without synchronized or instance-matched samples. Newly trained label-free receivers recover the requested addresses with 94.7% macro-average Address Realization Accuracy. We then attach the unseen SVHN dataset after freezing the base address space and train only lightweight adapters, achieving 90.2% MNIST>SVHN and 82.3% SVHN>MNIST end-to-end Address Transfer Accuracy without target-side decoder labels. To demonstrate generalizability of CIIR, we reuse the ten addresses as code elements in collision-free arithmetic and hierarchical address codes for all 100 CIFAR-100 classes and evaluate direct arithmetic-code realization through the existing receivers. These results establish CIIR as a compact interface for modular cross-dataset communication and post-hoc dataset attachment without exhaustive paired multimodal supervision.</p></details> | 17 pages, 6 figures |
| **[When to Align, When to Predict: A Phase Diagram for Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11190v4)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cross-modal alignment (CA) and cross-modal prediction (CP) are the dominant paradigms for multimodal representation learning, yet there is no systematic understanding of when each succeeds and when each fails, a gap that leaves practitioners, especially in scientific domains with heterogeneous instruments and multiple levels of measurement, unable to diagnose why standard methods underperform the best single modality. We study both objectives under a spiked signal-plus-noise model with structured cross-modal nuisance correlation, the ingredient that breaks the classical recovery guarantees, and derive separation ratios that expose complementary failure modes: alignment whitens each modality and fails when nuisance is strongly correlated across views; prediction encodes whatever is cross-predictable through a one-sided whitening, with recovery governed by source-modality quality. The resulting phase diagram partitions multimodal problems into four regimes --- Both, CA only, CP only, and Neither --- refined by a recovery count that separates partial recovery from complete failure. We present a data-driven procedure to locate real-world datasets in this diagram using a small labeled subsample, identifying the preferred objective and prediction direction before any cross-modal training, and identifying when no objective in the CA/CP family can improve on the stronger modality alone. Experiments on synthetic data, stereo-vision benchmarks, image--caption pairs, and two real scientific domains, astronomy and single-cell multi-omics, validate the predictions in the nonlinear regime, including both faces of the Neither regime. Code to reproduce the results is available at https://github.com/IlayMalinyak/mm_align_vs_pred.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond Invariance: Factor-wise Selective Treatment of Shifts for Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21403v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Distribution shifts pose a fundamental challenge to robust multimodal learning. Existing methods typically promote invariance by suppressing environment-dependent variations, implicitly applying a uniform treatment to shifted information. However, shifted information can have different predictive consequences: some contributes little to prediction, some remains reliably informative, and some exhibits environment-dependent predictive effects. This raises a fundamental question: which shifted information should be suppressed, preserved, or attenuated? To address this question, we propose FaShift, a factor-wise shift framework that diagnoses latent representation factors according to their distributional changes, intervention effects, and cross-environment stability. By factorizing multimodal representations and evaluating these complementary properties, FaShift distinguishes non-predictive factors, stably predictive factors, and predictively useful but unstable factors. It then selectively suppresses, preserves, or attenuates their contributions, rather than enforcing uniform invariance. Experiments on MOSI, MOSEI, and CH-SIMS show that FaShift achieves the best ID accuracy and F1 across all three datasets. Compared with the strongest competitors reporting OOD results, it improves OOD accuracy/F1 by 1.5/3.6 points on MOSI and 1.7/2.7 points on MOSEI, while achieving the best reported OOD accuracy and F1 on CH-SIMS.</p></details> |  |
| **[Task-Centric Multimodal Learning for Cognitive Impairment Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20906v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dementia affects millions of older adults worldwide, yet early detection remains difficult outside clinical settings. Traditional models primarily rely on text or voice-based responses, capturing only a limited view of human cognition despite the inherently multimodal nature of human expression. This work proposes a contactless, multimodal screening framework that leverages video, audio, and text signals collected during structured voice-based interactions with Amazon Alexa. By incorporating these modalities, our approach captures a more holistic representation of human behavior encompassing speech patterns, vocal characteristics, facial expressions, and linguistic structure, which may enable more accurate detection of cognitive decline. Different participants completed three conversational tasks designed to probe spontaneous speech, comprehension, and recall. We pursued three objectives: (1) evaluate which of the three Alexa tasks provides the most informative cognitive signals, (2) compare the effectiveness of unimodal models, and (3) assess the benefits of multimodal fusion models. Task-level analysis shows that the video model achieves its best performance on Command-Reading, with an accuracy of 0.65 (AUC 0.69), while the audio model performs best on Question-Answering, reaching an accuracy of 0.67 (AUC 0.73). In contrast, the multimodal model demonstrates consistently stable performance across tasks, achieving an accuracy of 0.76 (AUC 0.75) on Command-Reading and 0.76 (AUC 0.73) on Question-Answering. These findings demonstrate that multimodal behavioral cues captured through naturalistic interactions can reliably distinguish MCI from healthy aging, highlighting the limitations of traditional diagnostic paradigms and supporting the shift toward multimodal, real-world cognitive assessment frameworks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication at the 2026 IEEE Global Communications Conference (GLOBECOM)</p></details> |
| **[Instruction Mining From Images: Constructing a Synthetic Dataset for Multimodal Learning ILSP DCVLR Competition Submission](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19932v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This work presents a compact and reproducible pipeline for synthesizing and curating image-based instruction-response pairs for DCVLR. We draw from the Cauldron collection and use Magpie-style instruction and response synthesis with Qwen2.5-VL-7B Instruct to create concise, image-conditioned instruction pairs. The pipeline consists of instruction synthesis, conditioned on input images and controlled via a system prompt, sanitization, tagging, and filtering of the instructions based on the score obtained by a reward model (DeBERTa-v3). We retain samples with positive scores to maximize the quality of the generated instructions. We observe that score distributions have a median and mean reward score of ~ 1.53 and ~ 1.75 respectively, indicating that most generated instructions are of good quality. The resulting dataset contains diverse instructions in the math, spatial, and social reasoning domains.</p></details> |  |
| **[Towards Modality-Agnostic Seizure Classification: A Unified Deep Learning Framework for Heterogeneous EEG and Intracranial EEG Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16596v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Seizure prediction and detection, essential for clinical treatment of epilepsy, remain the crucial task of this neurological disease. EEG and iEEG are widely used to analyze epilepsy due to its capability of capturing discriminative brain activities at high time resolution. However, existing works generally analyze either EEG or iEEG seizure activity separately, where EEG analysis has been well-researched, while more and more works consider iEEG for its higher specificity at the spatial level and signal quality. Currently, most of the existing seizure prediction systems are single-modal in nature, which limits its application to a variety of clinical conditions. Therefore, a common framework to analyze seizures with both EEG and iEEG signals needs to be developed. This paper presents a new framework of multimodal seizure classification integrating the heterogeneous EEG and iEEG recordings from different subjects and sources. A specific framework, called Unified Multimodal Signal Harmonization (UMSH), is developed for harmonizing signals with varied sampling rates and recording conditions. Moreover, to handle the extremely class-imbalanced problem in the data sets, we use a Conditional Generative Adversarial Network (cGAN) based data augmentation technique to generate synthetic samples to balance the class distribution. The proposed framework uses the EEGNet-Vision Transformer (EEGNet-ViT) as the feature extraction and classification network to learn the local discriminative patterns as well as global contextual information in the multimodal seizure data. Experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework for robust seizure classification, emphasizing the potential of integrated EEG-iEEG multimodal learning in advancing next-generation epilepsy monitoring and clinical decision-support systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[More Than Seeing: Vision as Scalable Anchors in Native Multimodal Pre-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19443v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Native multimodal pre-training trains a single model from scratch on text and multimodal data, enabling it to understand both language and visual inputs. However, how visual signals shape scaling behavior and support capabilities beyond visual understanding remains unclear. We investigate this question through controlled visual-content ablations across model sizes and training budgets. Specifically, we replace image embeddings with content-free padding token embeddings. Retaining visual content improves text prediction on image-text examples without materially changing the lowest text-only loss at a given compute budget, the optimal balance of model size and training data, or aggregate language scores. The relative multimodal loss benefit grows approximately as a power law of compute. To test benefits beyond understanding images, we evaluate spatial reasoning from text descriptions alone. Both conditions improve with model and data scaling. During training of the largest model, visual gains are most sustained in localization, navigation, and symbolic planning, which involve maintaining object configurations or composing spatial updates; gains in spatial relations, geometry, and physical commonsense are less consistent. These findings identify visual content as a scalable anchor for multimodal learning, with benefits that extend beyond visual inputs while preserving aggregate language performance.</p></details> |  |
| **[VLX-Go: Robust Embodied Tracking through Multi-Stage Learning and On-Policy Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19361v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied visual tracking requires a robot to identify a designated target from visual observations and control its own motion to follow that target through an environment. Following an isolated person is a comparatively simple demonstration of embodied visual tracking, but following the intended person among distractors remains challenging. Existing methods exhibit a substantial drop in success from single-target to distractor tracking, motivating a focus on the shared spaces that person-following robots must navigate. We present \method{}, a vision--language waypoint policy built around the hypothesis that visual and language representations should be aligned before tracking-specific motion is learned. The policy combines complementary frozen visual encoders, multiscale temporal context, a transformer backbone, and a lightweight head for robot-centric waypoint prediction. Its training progresses through vision--language alignment, multimodal reasoning mid-training, and tracking specialization on 19,532 oracle episodes. Compared with 41.54\% distractor-tracking success for our earlier OmTrackVLA model, the complete multi-stage recipe achieves 55.80\%. We then explore on-policy refinement using filtered oracle labels for states visited by the policy. This preliminary study adds 2.42 percentage points of distractor-tracking success, reaching 58.22\%, while reducing collision rate by 1.99 pts. The final policy records the highest distractor- and ambiguous-tracking success rates among the compared methods. These results support staged multimodal learning as a promising direction for tracking among distractors and motivate further study of on-policy refinement for person following in shared spaces.</p></details> | tech report |
| **[Do More Modalities Always Help? A Geometric Perspective on Missing-Modality Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04792v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Missing modality remains a longstanding challenge in multimodal learning. Existing methods typically address this issue through modality recovery or adaptive strategies. However, they overlook models' internal cross-modal dependencies formed during multimodal training, which later impair robustness. We systematically characterize a counterintuitive deployment-time failure mode: models trained on full modalities can underperform unimodal models when one modality is missing at inference time. This pattern appears across diverse architectures, such as fusion models, CLIP-style two-tower models, and vision-language models. We show that such degradation is closely associated with learned cross-modal dependencies in the principal parameter subspaces. Multimodal training induces structured rotations of these subspaces, particularly in cross-modal interaction layers. These rotations are associated with reduced task-aligned margins and larger task-aware representation harm under missing-modality inputs. We propose Geodesic Unlearning (GU), a lightweight parameter-editing method that leverages Grassmannian subspace geometry for structured subspace correction to improve missing-modality robustness. It rotates the principal input subspace toward a unimodal reference along a geodesic path. We prove that this correction minimizes the distance to the reference within a fixed subspace-distance budget. Experiments across architectures and datasets show that GU improves performance under missing-modality inference while preserving full-modality accuracy, outperforming strong missing-modality robustness baselines. These findings support a geometric view of deployment-time missing-modality degradation and suggest localized subspace editing as a practical route for robustness correction.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026 Main Conference</p></details> |
| **[SYNPRED: A Synergistic Approach to Multimodal Learning for Clinical Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18110v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal medical data captures complementary aspects of disease. It provides a comprehensive basis for diagnosis and the identification of biological associations linked to progression or treatment response. Established contrastive learning approaches exploit shared information across modalities, but fall short in exploiting complementary, modality-specific signals relevant for a task. Here, we propose a synergistic multimodal variational autoencoder (VAE) framework that separates task-relevant unimodal and multimodal information from instance-specific variation task-irrelevant, allowing complementary signals across modalities to be leveraged for prediction. The method yields benefits even if modalities are missing at test time. We evaluate the method on a treatment response prediction task using medical imaging and RNA data. Empirical comparison with alignment-based and multimodal VAE state-of-the-art approaches demonstrates improved multimodal prediction performance, and consistent benefits for prediction from a single modality after multimodal training. The full code can be found here: https://github.com/cirmuw/SYNPRED.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond Forced Modality Balance: Intrinsic Information Budgets for Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17347v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal models often converge to a dominant-modality solution, in which a stronger, faster-converging modality overshadows weaker ones. This modality imbalance causes suboptimal performance. Existing methods attempt to balance different modalities by reweighting gradients or losses. However, they overlook the fact that each modality has finite information capacity. In this work, we propose IIBalance, a multimodal learning framework that aligns the modality contributions with Intrinsic Information Budgets (IIB). We propose a task-grounded estimator of each modality's IIB, transforming its capacity into a global prior over modality contributions. Anchored by the highest-budget modality, we design a prototype-based relative alignment mechanism that corrects semantic drift only when weaker modalities deviate from their budgeted potential, rather than forcing imitation. During inference, we propose a probabilistic gating module that integrates the global budgets with sample-level uncertainty to generate calibrated fusion weights. Experiments on three representative benchmarks demonstrate that IIBalance consistently outperforms state-of-the-art balancing methods and achieves better utilization of complementary modality cues. Our code is available at: https://github.com/XiongZechang/IIBalance.</p></details> | <details><summary>6 pag...</summary><p>6 pages, 4 figures, paper accepted by ICME 2026</p></details> |
| **[MODAIC: Coordinated Asynchrony for Decentralized Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16987v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal decentralized learning enables collaboration across heterogeneous edge devices without sharing raw data, but differing modality sets create coupled computation and communication bottlenecks. Multimodal agents support cross-modal alignment while training more model components and sharing bandwidth across multiple modality overlays. Under bandwidth limits, these agents can become hot spots: transfers miss aggregation windows, and their updates become stale or underrepresented, degrading worst-agent accuracy. Asynchrony removes global blocking but does not by itself coordinate local cross-modal interaction with timely peer-update exchange. We present MODAIC (Modality-Decoupled Asynchrony with Interleaved Coordination), a wait-free system that coordinates local modality progress, peer-to-peer update delivery, and aggregation under heterogeneous compute and bandwidth. Hierarchical asynchrony lets modalities progress independently while enforcing reciprocal feature exchange; load-aware update fetching overlaps training with communication, limits concurrent serving load, and reuses late arrivals; and consensus-aware peer selection uses lightweight metadata to account for update availability, peer participation, and loss similarity without active probing. We implement MODAIC as a containerized multi-agent framework and evaluate it on local and cross-region cloud testbeds. Across AVE and KU-HAR, MODAIC improves average accuracy and multimodal worst-agent performance. On AVE at 200 Mbps per agent, it improves multimodal worst-agent accuracy by 11.0 percentage points at 1000 seconds over the strongest baseline. On the cloud testbed, it reduces time-to-accuracy by up to 30%. Ablations show that simpler load-balancing and delayed-aggregation variants leave a worst-agent accuracy gap, and stress tests show tolerance to WAN variability and partial tracker unavailability.</p></details> |  |
| **[SpikeOcc: Towards Resilient Multimodal 3D Occupancy Perception via Spiking Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15745v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal 3D occupancy prediction underpins safe autonomous driving through comprehensive scene understanding. However, existing methods have largely pursued higher accuracy through sophisticated fusion, leaving resilience to missing sensors underexplored. In this paper, we investigate spiking neural networks (SNNs) as a means of building more resilient multimodal perception. Still, the discrete nature of spiking neurons makes optimization challenging. To overcome this difficulty, SpikeOcc is introduced with two complementary components, a Spiking Mixture-of-Experts (SMoE) and Sensor Availability Learning (SAL). Specifically, SMoE organizes RGB, LiDAR, and joint fusion into specialized pathways, enabling full-width unimodal representations while retaining the original multimodal features via a bounded residual. Beyond sensor dropout, SAL employs a staged curriculum with explicit routing supervision, progressively improving robustness under severe sensing conditions. Remarkably, SAL also yields notable gains for conventional models, highlighting its broader potential for resilient multimodal learning. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SpikeOcc maintains competitive full-sensor accuracy while markedly raising the performance floor under sensor outages. SpikeOcc illustrates the promise of neuromorphic computing, pointing toward more reliable and energy-efficient autonomous driving.</p></details> |  |
| **[Metric-Driven Reinforcement Learning for Object Hallucination Reduction in LVLM-Based Image Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14976v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) have excelled in joint visual and language understanding, particularly in image captioning. However, LVLMs remain prone to object hallucination, describing non-existent entities in the given image. Previous remedies, including dataset-enhanced supervised training and reinforcement learning from human feedback, show promise but rely heavily on costly human involvement, limiting scalability. To address this, we propose a reinforcement learning framework to mitigate object hallucination in LVLM-based image captioning, driven entirely by natural language processing (NLP) metrics. While optimizing reinforcement learning solely on NLP metrics offers scalability, it also introduces pitfalls: reward hacking, sparse feedback, and costly training. Our framework sidesteps these by introducing an Object Consistency Score (OCS) capturing both correctness and coverage of objects, incorporating KL-regularization for denser token-level guidance, and designing a lightweight PPO variant that fine-tunes adapters atop a frozen LVLM backbone, achieving efficiency and stability. Extensive experiments on the InstructBLIP baseline demonstrate the effectiveness and generalizability of our framework, achieving a substantial hallucination reduction of up to 41\% on the COCO dataset and 29\% on the Visual Genome. Furthermore, the framework can enhance caption quality by incorporating complementary evaluation metrics such as BERTScore and METEOR. These results reveal a broader insight in multimodal learning: LVLMs can effectively self-correct hallucinations using metric feedback, motivating deeper exploration into scalable reinforcement frameworks that significantly reduce human effort.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, IEEE ACCESS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Design Choices for Multimodal Transformer Learning with Missing and Longitudinal Healthcare Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14436v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal learning offers the potential to improve predictions by integrating complementary information from heterogeneous data sources. However, multimodal datasets are frequently incomplete, raising important questions about how models should handle missing information. Although recent approaches increasingly rely on Transformer-based architectures for multimodal fusion, incomplete multimodal data introduces challenges such as missing modalities and intra-modal missingness, which motivate key architectural design choices, such as tokenization granularity. In addition, many datasets are longitudinal, with observations collected across multiple time points. The sequential nature of the Transformer architectures provides a natural framework for modeling such data, but introduces additional considerations, such as how temporal information should be represented. In this study, we systematically investigate these design choices using three different cancer datasets. We compare modality-level and feature-level tokenization and evaluate attention masking and imputation as strategies for handling missing information, both naturally occurring in the datasets and synthetically induced. We further investigate approaches for incorporating temporal information in our training process and how to model it. Experiments are conducted with unimodal and multimodal configurations, as well as different levels of missingness for binary survival classification and multiple time encoding strategies for discrete-time survival prediction. Our results show that while multimodal outperforms unimodal results, missing data heavily impacts predictions and thus effective design choices such as tokenization granularity, and intra-missing handling is essential for reliable prediction.</p></details> |  |
| **[Linear Multi-granular Body Modeling for Text-Driven Motion Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.06897v3)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Text-to-motion generation is a rapidly growing field at the nexus of multimodal learning and computer graphics, enabling flexible and cost-effective applications in gaming, animation, robotics, and virtual reality. Existing part-aware pipelines typically split the body into segments and then recover whole-body coordination via attention-based fusion, which incurs quadratic complexity and fails to model part-level and whole-body cues simultaneously. Moreover, we observe that naively stacking spatial and temporal modules accumulates redundant features, thereby requiring deeper networks to achieve competitive fidelity. To address these issues, we propose HiSTF Mamba, a linear-time framework composed of Dual-Spatial Mamba, Bi-Temporal Mamba, and the Dynamic Spatiotemporal Fusion Module (DSFM). Dual-Spatial Mamba performs parallel part-based and whole-based modeling to simultaneously capture local joint dynamics and global coordination. Bi-Temporal Mamba employs bidirectional scanning to encode both short-term motion details and long-term dependencies. DSFM further removes redundancy and extracts complementary temporal information before fusing it with spatial features, yielding an expressive spatiotemporal representation. Extensive experiments on HumanML3D and KIT-ML demonstrate that HiSTF Mamba improves generation fidelity and semantic alignment while requiring substantially fewer parameters than existing segment-based architectures.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by ICME 2026</p></details> |
| **[Trajectory Prediction for Autonomous Driving in Road Construction Zones Using Human-in-the-Loop Driving Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12747v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Ego-trajectory prediction in urban roadway work zones remains an underexplored topic in autonomous-driving research, with limited dedicated data and systematic evaluation. Roadway work zones involve temporary changes in the lane layouts and connectivity of the roads, complicating interactions with surrounding vehicles and workers. These changes make future vehicle trajectories difficult to predict. To address these challenges, we collect WZDrive-Data, a multimodal dataset comprising 1,183 work-zone driving segments from 33 drivers, using a human-in-the-loop driving simulator with prerecorded worker animations and VR-controlled workers. Driver control and gaze signals are recorded from human participants, while vehicle trajectories and scene information are obtained from the simulator. Our proposed multimodal ego-trajectory predictor, WZDrive, achieves a mean minADE (K=6) of 1.09 m and minFDE (K=6) of 2.12 m. Compared with the best-performing state-of-the-art baseline for each metric in our benchmark, WZDrive reduces minADE (K=6) by 7.18% and minFDE (K=6) by 13.18%. This research addresses a gap in autonomous-driving research on urban work zones by combining human-in-the-loop data collection with multimodal learning from human driving signals and in the context of work zones.</p></details> |  |
| **[MSGAT: Multi-Head Spiking Graph Attention with Similarity-Space Fusion for Image-Text Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11526v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spiking neural networks (SNNs) offer an energy-efficient computing paradigm through sparse event-driven computation, showing great potential for efficient multimodal learning. However, applying SNNs to high-level multimodal tasks, such as image-text retrieval (ITR), remains challenging, since sparse spike representations make it difficult to capture semantic structures required for cross-modal alignment. Existing spiking ITR methods rely on local alignment and additional soft-label supervision during training, while lacking awareness of structural and multi-granularity relationships. To address these issues, we propose a Multi-head Spiking Graph Attention Network (\textbf{MSGAT}) for structural modeling and equip it with dynamic attention heads to capture complementary relational patterns and enable spike-driven graph reasoning and aggregation. However, within a two-branch multi-granularity fusion framework, the fine-grained spike representations generated by MSGAT are sparse and discrete, whereas the global representations are continuous, making conventional feature-level fusion susceptible to interference across heterogeneous representations. Therefore, we introduce \textbf{Sim-Fuse}, a similarity-space fusion alignment strategy integrating coarse- and fine-grained matching relations while avoiding direct fusion of heterogeneous representations. Experiments on Flickr30K and MSCOCO show our method outperforms ANN methods under matched settings and existing SNN retrieval baselines. Moreover, with only two time steps, our SNN achieves comparable or superior performance to its ANN counterpart while reducing theoretical module-level energy by 55\%. The code is provided in the Supplementary Materials.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learning What to Trust in Multimodal Learning under Noisy Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11057v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal classification processes and relates information from multiple modalities to achieve more accurate predictions. However, existing methods typically rely on high-quality ground-truth labels, which are difficult to obtain in real-world scenarios. While sample-selection methods for learning with noisy labels aim to identify correctly labeled examples from noisy data, traditional methods primarily focus on unimodal settings and fail to exploit multimodal information fully. This motivates us to build a more reliable noise detector in multimodal learning. To this end, we theoretically analyze the relationship between representation structure and noise detection capability. Based on this analysis, we propose REFINE, which is a multimodal label-noise detection framework that jointly uses fused and unimodal representations for label-noise detection. Specifically, REFINE constructs discriminative eigenvectors through discriminative analysis of the target and background classes and selects trusted representation spaces with better noise detection capability for each class. Within each trusted space, REFINE measures the alignment between each instance representation and the discriminative eigenvectors. It then combines the subsets selected from these spaces. The combined set provides cleaner supervision for updating the multimodal classifier, thereby reducing the influence of mislabeled examples during training and improving model generalization. Extensive experiments across diverse tasks demonstrate REFINE's superiority compared to baseline methods. The source code will be publicly available.</p></details> |  |
| **[PXtal: Learning to Align Powder X-Ray Diffraction and Crystal Structures under Information Asymmetry across Modalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10653v1)** | 2026-10-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scientific multimodal learning commonly assumes that paired views are comparably informative. Powder X-ray diffraction (PXRD) makes this mismatch explicit: compressing a three-dimensional crystal structure into a one-dimensional diffraction pattern loses information and makes the pattern harder to connect to the crystal structure that produced it. We introduce PXtal, a framework for learning aligned PXRD and crystal representations under this physically imposed information asymmetry. PXtal uses Unbalanced Optimal Transport (UOT) to adapt the cross-modal coupling and coupling-level generalized Kullback-Leibler (GKL) divergence to supervise the full transport plan. Across six test sets, including four zero-shot transfer sets, PXtal consistently outperforms the baseline models in PXRD-to-crystal candidate retrieval, with the largest gains when PXRD patterns have close but crystallographically distinct nonpaired neighbors, meaning similar input patterns associated with different crystals. The resulting crystal and PXRD encoders transfer more effectively to downstream materials and crystallographic tasks. These results identify information asymmetry as a general design problem in scientific multimodal learning: alignment objectives should reflect what each modality preserves.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 2 figures, 14 tables</p></details> |
| **[Leakage-Controlled Multimodal Learning for Diagnosis and Progression Prediction in Alzheimer's Disease Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10648v1)** | 2026-10-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Alzheimer's disease prediction involves irregular visits, heterogeneous measurements and incomplete modalities. This study presents a multimodal multitask framework combining an adapted SFCN MRI encoder, four causal clinical Transformers, shared fusion and task-specific ODE-GRU dynamics. Fine-tuning and LoRA adapt the final two MRI blocks. Task-DRO balances task losses, while Group-CVaR targets cohort and comorbidity strata. Branch-specific input controls, subject-grouped partitions and empirical causality checks support longitudinal evaluation. Across 2,649 subjects and 17,317 visits from ADNI, OASIS-2 and MIRIAD, internal validation yields diagnosis, stage-1 progression and first-stage-1-visit progression AUROCs of 0.935 +/- 0.002, 0.884 +/- 0.003 and 0.870 +/- 0.005, respectively (mean +/- SD across three seeds). Corresponding hybrid AUROCs are 0.951, 0.909 and 0.896. Next-visit MMSE mean absolute error (MAE) is 1.61 points; worst-stratum diagnosis AUROC is 0.827 +/- 0.008. Sampled ADNI explanations identify task-specific input dependence. OASIS-3 external validation yields network and hybrid diagnosis AUROCs of 0.763 and 0.767, hybrid next-visit progression AUROC of 0.764, diagnosis calibration error decreasing from 0.197 to 0.052, and next-visit MMSE MAE of 0.86. Seed-42 paired ablations of six components yield pooled diagnosis and progression AUROC differences between -0.004 and +0.004; removing clinical encoder inputs lowers diagnosis AUROC by 0.272. The framework integrates longitudinal prediction, missing-modality handling, auxiliary comorbidity modelling and subgroup evaluation within a common pipeline.</p></details> |  |
| **[FedSSMCoOp: SSM Encoders for light-weight Federated Prompt Learning for Few-shot Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09907v1)** | 2026-10-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have shown strong performance across a wide range of downstream vision tasks, thanks to the complementary information contained in the respective domains. This is especially critical in biomedical applications under federated constraints, where data sharing is restricted, labeled data is scarce at each site, and it differs widely across institutions, leading to substantial statistical heterogeneity. To overcome this issue, we propose FedSSMCoOp, a federated few-shot image classification framework that enables multimodal learning while preserving data privacy. With the help of the SSM-based Vision Mamba and Cross Mamba blocks, and by optimizing only the soft-prompt and communication-prompt updates in the federated setting, the framework prioritizes both computation and performance. Importantly, this eliminates the need to use an external Large Language Model (LLM) for feature alignment. | **[Batch Augmentation with Unimodal Fine-tuning for Multimodal Fusion of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06592v2)** | 2026-10-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this paper, we propose batch augmentation with unimodal fine-tuning for multimodal learning. Depending on the data, some unimodal models can be replaced by hard-coded scripts or AI agents. The unimodal training can also follow batch augmentation when the data is augmentable. According to our literature search, the proposed method achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) accuracy of 93.29% on the UPMC Food-101 dataset, while we apply the ViT-L/16 model for vision and the GPT-2 model for text. | **[Dynamic Alignment and Calibration for Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07928v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dynamic multimodal learning aims to learn robust representations by adaptively modeling information discrepancies across modalities. To address these issues, we propose an Alignment- and Calibration-driven Multimodal Learning framework (ACML). Specifically, ACML incorporates a dynamic cross-modal triplet alignment module, which enforces strong semantic consistency for high-confidence positive pairs while encouraging diverse representation learning between high- and low-confidence positive pairs according to their confidence gaps. Additionally, ACML introduces a difference-aware attention calibration strategy that adaptively adjusts attention regularization based on feature magnitude and confidence differences across modalities, thereby mitigating biases caused by unreasonable fusion constraints. | **[Constant-Curvature Sliced Gromov-Wasserstein for Heterogeneous Cross-Curvature Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07218v1)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in representation learning have highlighted the utility of constant-curvature models, such as hyperbolic and spherical spaces, for modeling complex data. Mixed-curvature models further enhance this by integrating multiple constant-curvature components. Consequently, they lack explicit mechanisms to enforce geometric consistency across various spaces. Moreover, the problem of comparing probability distributions across mixed-curvature spaces remains unexplored. To compare distributions on heterogeneous spaces, Gromov-Wasserstein (GW) distances provide a principled framework by aligning their intra-space geometries. Building on this, we propose constant-curvature sliced Gromov-Wasserstein (CCSGW), a novel divergence for aligning distributions supported on heterogeneous constant-curvature spaces. We first introduce the missing geodesic-based one-dimensional projections for spherical spaces, and then extend sliced GW to constant-curvature spaces, enabling efficient and principled comparison across manifolds with different curvatures. This formulation preserves intrinsic geometric relationships while avoiding the high computational cost. We provide theoretical analysis showing that CCSGW controls intrinsic geometric discrepancy across heterogeneous spaces, promoting distribution-level geometric consistency. By integrating CCSGW into existing mixed-curvature learning tasks, including graph anomaly detection, graph node classification, and multimodal learning, we observe consistent performance gains across diverse settings.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Wearable Multimodal Ultrasound+Inertial System for Real-Time Virtual Reality Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.17741v3)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A-mode ultrasound (US) is a promising sensing modality for Virtual Reality (VR) interaction, as it enables the mapping of muscular activity into control commands while retaining the benefits of wearable sensing. The system is built on the WULPUS platform and integrates an end-to-end software framework for real-time acquisition, visualization, and communication with a Unity-based VR environment. A multimodal learning pipeline is introduced for concurrent hand pose and forearm position estimation in 2D space. The interface is evaluated through offline and online experiments with five subjects, during the execution of three functional tasks: cylinder grasping (gross motor) and relocation, marble pinching (fine motor) and relocation, and liquid pouring. For offline experiments, we collect 5 acquisition sessions across multiple days, achieving an average inter-session accuracy across subjects of 80$\pm$6\% for hand pose estimation and 77$\pm$7\% for forearm position estimation. | **[Fitting Vision Adapters at Frontier Scales](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05897v1)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Training a small projector between a frozen vision encoder and language model is an established approach to multimodal learning. | **[InstMoE: Adaptive Multimodal Routing with Specialized Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05111v1)** | 2026-10-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal inputs are inherently heterogeneous, not only across modalities but also in the information pathways required for effective prediction. At the data level, each client trains a lightweight local generative replay module to synthesize raw-data-free embedding-level multimodal replay tuples without any raw data sharing. | **[GeoBalance: Geometry-Aware Monitoring and Reconstruction with Asymmetric Optimization for Balanced Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23533v1)** | 2026-09-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal classifiers can converge to modality-dominant solutions in which one modality dominates the joint prediction, suppressing the learning of others. | **[From Models to Systems: A Comprehensive Survey of Efficient Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19445v1)** | 2026-09-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid expansion of multimodal models has surfaced formidable bottlenecks in computation, memory, and deployment, catalyzing the rise of Efficient Multimodal Learning (EML) as a pivotal research frontier. Furthermore, we provide a holistic discussion and application-specific optimization blueprints for diverse domains and posit a paradigm shift toward self-regulating intelligence, where efficiency is an intrinsic, emergent property of the model's fundamental design rather than a post-hoc constraint. | **[Semantics at an Angle: When Cosine Similarity Works Until It Doesn't](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16318v4)** | 2026-08-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cosine similarity is a standard comparison rule for learned representations in information retrieval, natural language processing, computer vision, and multimodal learning. Its popularity is well founded: it removes positive radial scale, is computationally convenient, and often matches objectives that train normalized embeddings. These same properties also delimit what cosine can express. Normalization discards radial variation; anisotropic representations can compress angular contrast; high-dimensional neighborhoods can develop hubs; and a symmetric, uncalibrated score may be mismatched to the relation of interest. This article offers a selective review organized around a simple principle: the usefulness of cosine similarity depends jointly on the learned representation, any normalization or post-processing, the scoring rule, and the downstream decision. We derive the main geometric identities, distinguish failure mechanisms that are often conflated, review representative evidence about embedding norms, and describe objective-matched, geometry-aware, hubness-aware, norm-aware, and learned alternatives. The central conclusion is conditional rather than adversarial: cosine's positive-scale invariance is justified when radial variation is nuisance or fixed by the representation contract, but its angular geometry and downstream decision must still be validated.</p></details> |  |

| **[SAGG: Sample-Adaptive Gradient Gating for Robust Multimodal Learning under Heterogeneous Corruption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20302v1)** | 2026-07-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal gradient balancing methods modulate encoder gradients with a shared scalar per modality, implicitly assuming that corruption is uniform across the training batch. ## Representation Learning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Structural Causal Bottleneck Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08682v3)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce structural causal bottleneck models (SCBMs), a novel class of structural causal models in which causal effects between high-dimensional variables are mediated by low-dimensional summary statistics, or *bottlenecks*. SCBMs provide a flexible framework for mechanism-specific, target-dependent dimension reduction while remaining estimable via standard learning algorithms. We prove that bottleneck variables are identifiable up to bijection from observational data, and validate this experimentally. We then study causal effect estimation in linear SCBMs under three standard adjustment strategies. For instrumental variables, we identify a population-level non-identifiability of naïve two-stage least squares when all variables are high-dimensional, and show that bottleneck representations restore identification. For mediation and backdoor adjustment, we prove that conditioning on bottleneck variables yields valid effect estimates with explicit finite-sample efficiency gains over conditioning on full high-dimensional variables. We argue that SCBMs provide a principled alternative to existing causal dimension reduction frameworks such as causal representation learning and causal abstractions.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[Synthetic Image Oversampling for Rare-Class Representation Learning in Deep Facial Phenotyping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23730v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent deep facial phenotyping methods can support the recognition of genetic syndromes, but patient images are often scarce and unevenly distributed across disorders. It is therefore necessary to examine both how much synthetic augmentation to use and which disorders benefit. We investigate disease-specific synthetic oversampling using images from the Phenotypic Disease Image Database to augment a subset of the GestaltMatcher Database. We compare increasing augmentation levels in an image-based model, GestaltMatcher-Arc, and a multimodal model, GestaltMML, while keeping the real training and patient-disjoint evaluation partitions fixed. Repeated training runs assess overall and disease-specific accuracy, and an exploratory embedding analysis examines changes in class compactness and separation. Augmentation improves mean recognition accuracy in both training setups, although gains remain modest in GestaltMatcher-Arc and continue across the tested levels in GestaltMML. These aggregate improvements are accompanied by uneven disease-level responses, including reduced accuracy for some disorders. In the embedding analysis, disorders with fewer real training images tend to gain more class separation, but geometric changes do not consistently track classification performance. Together, the findings support synthetic oversampling for limited-data facial phenotyping and emphasize the need to assess its amount and benefit for individual disorders within each training setup.</p></details> | <details><summary>BIBM ...</summary><p>BIBM 2026 ML4BMI Workshop</p></details> |
| **[Learning Cross-Coupled and Regime Dependent Dynamics for Aerial Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14805v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Accurate dynamics modeling of aerial manipulators is challenging because platform--manipulator coupling, history-dependent interactions, and changing payload or manipulator configurations produce cross-coupled, temporal, and nonstationary residual dynamics. We propose a structured encoder--decoder framework in which a nonlinear history encoder captures temporal and cross-variable dependencies, while a lightweight linear latent decoder supports efficient deployment-time adaptation. Its linear-in-parameter structure enables closed-form Bayesian updates with consistency-driven covariance inflation and integration with real-time model predictive control (MPC). Experiments on a real aerial-manipulation platform demonstrate improved residual-prediction accuracy, rapid adaptation following dynamics changes, and lower MPC tracking error under changing payload conditions. The results show that combining structured history-dependent representation learning with lightweight online adaptation provides an effective predictive model for aerial manipulation under coupled and nonstationary dynamics.</p></details> |  |

Training accuracy measures agreement with training labels but provides little information about representation geometry. We use persistent homology to track loop structure across distance thresholds in neural activations. Experiments span image classification, text classification, and modular arithmetic. On a fixed CIFAR-10/ResNet-18 training probe, equally sized clean-label and corrupted-label subsets show no detectable persistence difference at epoch 10, whereas corrupted-label subsets have higher persistence at epoch 50. The contrast survives uniform-scale controls, even though predictions match the assigned noisy labels for only 7-11% of corrupted probe examples at that checkpoint. Normalisation preserves the crossover between clean and noisy training trajectories on CIFAR-10/ResNet-18, whereas CIFAR-100/ResNet-18 shows no crossover under any of the five evaluated constructions. In a separate noise-rate calibration comparison across five pipelines, mean activation norm achieves a higher mean in-sample R2 than raw persistence (0.936 versus 0.908). These findings show that noisy supervision leaves a measurable geometric signature even when agreement with corrupted labels is low. Comparing persistence under scale controls with label-fitting measurements offers a way to study how representations respond to label corruption beyond what training accuracy reveals.</p></details> |  |

To address this limitation, we investigate a human-inspired evaluation framework that avoids representations learned for unrelated tasks. Specifically, we train a generator-specific classifier to distinguish real images from samples produced by a given model and analyze its decisions using saliency maps. Across diverse benchmarks, we observe that these classifiers learn generator-specific artifacts rather than a universal notion of "fakeness", exhibiting strong accuracy and limited cross-generator transfer. This makes saliency analysis particularly informative: the resulting attribution maps highlight visual cues and structural irregularities characteristic of each model's outputs. By combining classification accuracy and saliency information, we obtain a composite score aligned with human judgments, without relying on large pretrained backbones and using only real and generated samples from the evaluated model.</p></details> |  |
| **[Who Can Change Course? An Integrative Review of AI Sovereignty and Collective Learning Capacity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00729v3)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper examines AI sovereignty as national learning capacity, using France as an application. Human-Centered Learning Mechanics (HCLM), proposed for entropy-regulated representation learning (Tran, 2026), provides a conceptual lens for relating information inputs, absorptive capacity and avoidable institutional losses. Inputs include compute, data, talent, research, capital, deployment and institutional experimentation. Potential losses include coordination failures, administrative duplication, energy constraints, regulatory uncertainty and limited organizational uptake. Regulation also supports learning through evaluation, trusted data access, interoperability and safety standards. The argument is that sovereignty depends on converting distributed resources and knowledge into usable, coordinated and responsibly evaluated capability. The analysis connects this account with neural scaling laws, endogenous growth, creative destruction and standard game-theoretic mechanisms. It develops conditional scaling relationships, policy indicators and illustrative scenarios, alongside descriptive OECD, IMF and World Bank evidence. Country observations are kept separate from assumed scenario values, and a fully specified public-goods example shows how decentralized incentives can limit shared provision. The framework offers a research heuristic rather than a literal transfer of neural-network equations to countries or an established measure of sovereignty. By imposing a strictly upper triangular constraint on the transition matrix, we mathematically ensure numerical stability and enforce a robust polynomial boundary that effectively captures long-term dependencies while preventing the exponential divergence typically found in unconstrained ODE-based models. H</p></details> |

Overall, the study shifts value assessment from AI-generated products to skills, providing a foundation for pricing and optimizing AI-augmented skill portfolios.</p></details> | <details><summary>74 pa...</summary><p>74 pages, under review in Journal of Marketing Research</p></details> |
| **[What is causal about causal models and representations?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.19335v3)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Causal Bayesian networks are often described as causal because they support reasoning about interventions. Yet interventions are defined in the mathematical model, whereas the resulting model predictions concern the effects of actions in the real world. Consequently, whether and how a causal model makes falsifiable predictions about the effects of actions depends on how actions are interpreted as interventions. This requires specifying which actions correspond to which interventions, a step that is frequently left implicit. We develop a formal framework that separates a causal model from the data-generating process it aims to describe and formalizes interpretations connecting actions to interventions. Within this framework, we prove an impossibility result: no interpretation can simultaneously satisfy a collection of desirable properties while remaining non-circular. Under a circular interpretation, every model that fits the observational distribution is interventionally valid, so no experiment can falsify it. We analyze several non-circular alternatives and discuss implications for falsification, causal discovery, causal representation learning, and causal abstraction. More broadly, our framework provides a way to make assumptions about the connections between actions and interventions explicit and to reason formally about the empirical content of causal models.</p></details> |  |
| **[ODSS-Net: Orthogonally Decoupled Shared-Specific Representations for Multi-Modal Object Re-Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22922v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-modal object re-identification (ReID) leverages complementary information from heterogeneous sensors. To address these issues, we propose ODSS-Net, a framework for orthogonally decoupled shared-specific representation learning and adaptive fusion of complementary modality-specific features. First, a Shared Structural Feature Construction (SSFC) module suppresses modality-dependent appearance variations and extracts modality-shared structural information. Then, an Orthogonal Specific Feature Learning (OSFL) module aggregates identity-relevant local information and removes the shared-aligned component via geometric orthogonal projection. Finally, a Sample Adaptive Mixture of Experts (SAMoE) module uses the shared structure as routing context to adaptively refine and fuse modality-specific features. Experiments on three benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of ODSS-Net for multi-modal object ReID.</p></details> | <details><summary>ICASS...</summary><p>ICASSP 2027 conference submission</p></details> |
| **[Learning to Be Guided: Shaping Latent Geometry for Classifier-Free Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22885v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Classifier-free guidance (CFG) is a standard component of conditional diffusion models, and guided generation performance has become the \textit{de facto} measure of state-of-the-art models. Under a Gaussian class model, we show that shifting variance from intra- to inter-class at fixed total variance makes classes more distinguishable under noise, and derive a noisy posterior objective whose per-sample descent direction is proportional to the oracle CFG correction. Based on the analysis, we propose Prototype Regularization (PR), a lightweight surrogate for the intractable noisy posterior objective, which applies a cosine prototype classifier to spatially pooled latents. With a controlled training budget (20 epochs for VAE, 80 epochs for DiT), adding PR to an LDM baseline reduces guided gFID on ImageNet from 3.31 to 2.28, surpassing a DINOv3-aligned VA-VAE (2.44) with negligible overhead. We further demonstrate that PR generalizes to pseudo-labels derived from clustering, continuous text-embedding conditions, and video latents. Together, our findings highlight class geometry as a critical factor in guided generation and take a first step toward CFG-compatible representation learning. | **[MOSAIC: Module Discovery via Sparse Additive Identifiable Causal Learning for Scientific Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05524v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Causal representation learning (CRL) seeks to recover latent variables with identifiability guarantees, typically up to permutation and component-wise reparameterization under appropriate assumptions. In contrast, scientific observations (such as residue-pair distances, climate indices, or process sensors) are inherently semantic, as they correspond to named physical quantities. We show that ANOVA main-effect supports are identifiable under general smooth mixing functions, with finite-sample recovery guarantees for a group-lasso estimator and empirical evaluation of MOSAIC's entropy regularizer. Experiments on the mainstream benchmark across multiple prediction horizons and different temporal models show that Sera consistently improves forecasting performance, achieving up to a 37.3% reduction in prediction error over the temporal baseline and enhanced generalizability. Counterfactual analysis examines how forecasts respond to changes in degradation semantics to assess interpretability. The results show that prediction responses are consistent with the meanings of key degradation descriptors across tested horizons. Together, these findings demonstrate that structured degradation semantics and effective aggregation can improve forecasting accuracy and support reliable and interpretable battery health forecasting for advanced battery management.</p></details> |  |

## Causal Inference
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Testing Treatment Effect Heterogeneity with Functional Outcomes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23397v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Assessing treatment-effect heterogeneity is important in causal inference, particularly for functional outcomes where effects may vary over time and the common effect is unknown. We propose a nonparametric procedure that assesses treatment-effect homogeneity through a testable marginal implication. By comparing within-arm difference distributions, the construction removes the unknown common functional effect without estimating it. We measure departures between these distributions through an integrated squared discrepancy of their characteristic functionals. A full-support Gaussian integrating measure yields an explicit bounded-kernel representation and identifies equality of the difference laws without requiring moment conditions on the outcomes. We construct an unbiased generalized U-statistic that accounts for dependence among overlapping differences, derive its null, fixed-alternative, and local-alternative limits, and develop a centered multiplier bootstrap for inference. The framework extends to multiple treatment arms with a shared control. For computation, a random-direction approximation combined with a nine-state recursion reduces the cost to linear order in the sample size for fixed numerical settings. Simulation studies assess the finite-sample performance of the proposed procedure relative to several benchmark methods. An application to the Irish gas smart-metering trial illustrates its use for assessing heterogeneity in functional treatment responses.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages. Includes brief proof outlines; detailed technical proofs and supplementary numerical/empirical appendices are not included in this preprint version</p></details> |
| **[DeDiCa: A Scalable Deconfounding Discrete Causal Generative Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23272v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce DeDiCa, a practical framework for efficient causal inference with discrete variables and hidden confounding that revisits classical probabilistic modeling through a tensorized parameterization of causal Bayesian networks, enabling tractable inference. Despite this deliberately simple parameterization, DeDiCa matches state-of-the-art neural causal generative models in ATE estimation, while supporting memory-efficient CPU training and exact interventional inference in milliseconds. Compared with causal foundation models, DeDiCa scales more effectively with additional observational samples and achieves competitive accuracy at substantially lower inference time and memory. DeDiCa provides a tractable, accurate, and scalable approach to causal inference in discrete settings with hidden confounding.</p></details> | <details><summary>32 pa...</summary><p>32 pages, 14 figures, 7 tables</p></details> |

Specifically, we develop identifiability conditions and show that past and future observations can be employed as natural proxies for unmeasured confounders. We further develop the bridge function required for valid PCI estimation along with asymptotic variance. Simulation studies illustrate our method's validity and robustness to violation of certain PCI identification assumptions. We demonstrate the potential for studying socio-environmental exposure health effects by applying our method to a smartphone study of bipolar patients. Our work contributes to the growing literature on PCI and provides a powerful tool for analyzing longitudinal data, including in observational mobile health research prone to confounding bias.</p></details> |  |

| **[Graph Dirichlet Energy for Fitting-Free Bivariate Causal Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21892v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We propose the Graph Dirichlet Energy Causal Inference (GDECI) criterion for distinguishing cause from effect in bivariate settings. The score is the Dirichlet energy of this signal, induced by the unnormalized graph Laplacian. The reverse score swaps the variables, and the lower-energy direction is inferred as causal. The method requires neither regression fitting nor independence testing. After the empirical probability integral transform, its decisions are invariant under strictly monotone transformations of either variable, and the graph operations reduce to convolutions, allowing exact evaluation in $O(n\log n)$ time and $O(n)$ memory. At the population level, GDECI compares degree-weighted Bayes squared prediction errors plus a non-negative smoothness term of the regression function. On the real-world Tuebingen pairs, it ranked third in unweighted accuracy ($0.684$) and fifth in weighted accuracy ($0.678$). It was also the fastest of the compared methods up to $n\approx10^{4}$. | **[Causal Bayesian Optimization: Foundations, Methods, and Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24112v1)** | 2026-09-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Causal Bayesian Optimization (CBO) combines causal inference with Bayesian optimization to enable sample-efficient intervention selection in systems with causal structure. We also introduce a reproducibility-oriented benchmark spanning hard- and soft-intervention settings, with standardized GAP and a new trajectory-aware Path-Aware GAP (PA-GAP), evaluating seven CBO methods and a non-causal BO baseline across thirteen datasets, three budgets, and two metrics. We conclude by identifying key open challenges, including robustness to causal-assumption violations, scalable unknown-graph optimization, mixed intervention types, realistic cost models, stronger theoretical guarantees, and integration with modern representation learning and causal abstractions.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at Transactions on Machine Learning Research (TMLR), 2026</p></details> |
| **[Exponential Family Synthetic Controls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23970v1)** | 2026-09-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We develop exponential family synthetic controls (EFSC), a distributional version of synthetic controls for a panel of datasets. | **[tteICE: An R Package for Estimating Treatment Effects on Time-to-Event Outcomes with Intercurrent Events in Two-Arm Trials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23473v1)** | 2026-09-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluating treatment effects in clinical trials with time-to-event outcomes is complicated by intercurrent events (ICEs), e.g., treatment discontinuation or competing events. This article introduces the \pkg{tteICE} package for \proglang{R}, which implements five strategies to facilitate valid causal inference in the presence of ICEs. \pkg{tteICE} enables researchers to either nonparametrically or semiparametrically efficiently estimate potential cumulative incidence functions under both treatment and control conditions. We situate this perspective relative to complementary approaches for unobserved confounding. Together, these ideas provide a bridge between mixture learning and causal inference, connecting recent advances in high-dimensional mixture learning to scalable identification and estimation of causal effects while raising new challenges for mixture learning.</p></details> |  |

OSIP is based on two-stage optimization algorithm. In both stages it considers a projection of the (high-dimensional) data into one-dimensional space by evaluating an estimated propensity score for each unit. The method is based on enforcing a partition of the input to subsets of consecutive intervals of estimated propensity scores. The novelty arises because we consider this task as a global optimization problem. In the first stage, we employ an optimal partitioning algorithm with respect to an auxiliary (one-dimensional) goal function. In the second phase, we go back to the original distance function and apply heuristics approaches locally optimizing the solution from the first stage. We compare our framework with established methodologies from the literature across well-studied empirical benchmarks. We use the standard benchmark of Lindner cardiovascular dataset to demonstrate that OSIP provides a highly efficient sweet spot solution for the corresponding multi-criteria goal. | **[Pretrained Medical Representations for the Practical Screening of Drug Repositioning Candidates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19865v1)** | 2026-09-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Representation learning from medical code sequences in electronic health records and medical claims data has been successful in various clinical applications, such as those regarding disease prediction. ## LLM
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[A Large Language Model LLM Approach to COVID-19 Fake News Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23800v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Text classification is a widely studied problem in information retrieval and data mining, with applications ranging from healthcare diagnosis to the detection of COVID-19-related fake news. Social media platforms generate vast volumes of user-generated text, and the rapid spread of misinformation during the pandemic has made automated fake news detection an important task. In this study, we apply pretrained transformer-based transfer learning (TL) models and large language models (LLMs), namely BERT-base, ALBERT-base, XLM-RoBERTa-base, DistilBERT, GPT-2, XLNet, and T5, to the COVID-19 English Tweets dataset, which contains 6,420 tweets labeled as real or fake. To further improve performance, we apply six late-fusion optimization techniques: weighted fusion, particle swarm optimization (PSO) fusion, the Nelder-Mead algorithm, Powell's method, the Broyden-Fletcher-Goldfarb-Shanno (BFGS) algorithm, and trust-region constrained optimization. Models are evaluated using accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score, and confusion-matrix heatmaps are used to visualize the predictions of each model. GPT-2 achieved the best performance, with an accuracy of 0.9631, precision of 0.9612, recall of 0.9610, and F1-score of 0.9600, while Nelder-Mead fusion achieved the best result among the fusion methods with an accuracy of 0.9610. These results highlight the value of LLMs for COVID-19 fake news detection. We conclude by outlining future directions, including multiclass classification, multilingual datasets, and real-time social media streams.</p></details> | <details><summary>Pages...</summary><p>Pages 15, 10-15 Figures</p></details> |
| **[Revisiting Cauchy-Schwarz Norm Bounds for SDC Detection in LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18557v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) execute billions of floating-point operations per output token across many hardware accelerators. At this scale, hardware-induced faults can bypass system-level detection mechanisms and cause Silent Data Corruption (SDC). Algorithm-Based Fault Tolerance (ABFT) is the state-of-the-art technique for protecting matrix multiplication from such faults, but it incurs non-negligible computational overhead and requires thresholds to distinguish hardware-induced errors from floating-point roundoff. In this paper, we revisit Cauchy--Schwarz Norm Bounds (CSNB) as a low-overhead alternative for detecting SDC during LLM inference. We compare CSNB against ABFT through controlled fault injection and evaluate the runtime overhead of both verification and threshold determination across multiple model sizes, batch sizes, and generation lengths. Our results show that, on average, CSNB retains 99% of the fault detection capability of ABFT for the evaluated linear layers in dense LLMs, while reducing verification overhead by 87% and threshold determination overhead by 67%. For Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) router gate computations, however, CSNB retains only 55% of the fault detection capability of ABFT. These findings suggest that CSNB is a promising low-overhead alternative for protecting linear layers in dense LLMs, while checksum-based verification remains preferable for the evaluated MoE router gate.</p></details> |  |
| **[LLM-Assisted Bi-Approximate CHC Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23771v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Constrained Horn clause (CHC) solvers often struggle to discover deep counterexamples whose derivations may require extensive rule unfolding. Although large language models (LLMs) can propose long-range symbolic relations, prior work on formally checked LLM-assisted verification has mainly focused on generating inductive invariants and concrete traces. We exploit this broader capability through Derivation Power Under-approximations (DPUs). Each DPU is a solver-certified under-approximation of the relation induced by a multi-step CHC derivation. An LLM proposes each DPU with symbolic intermediate-state witnesses that enable SMT validation without introducing additional quantifiers. Validated DPUs can be composed and reused soundly. Building on this mechanism, our LLM-assisted framework HornCraft combines invariant generation for over-approximation with DPU generation for under-approximation in CHC solving. On 260 linear and nonlinear integer-arithmetic CHC-COMP benchmarks left unresolved by a 30-minute Spacer run, HornCraft returns certified verdicts for 228 instances. It certifies 42 instances as unsafe, compared with 22 when DPU construction and reuse are disabled.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 8 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[Recognizing Study Lineage Is Not Enough: Over-Crediting Same-Trial Evidence in LLM-Based Medical Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23721v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Publication count is not study count. A model can identify shared trial lineage correctly in a direct task yet over-credit a second publication from the same trial when aggregating evidence. We evaluate this behavior in medical LLM verification using 14 source-audited clinical families and five model conditions. The families span provenance, clinical applicability, result direction, and composition; the provenance families provide the direct lineage tests. Recognition and aggregation are separate stateless calls, and repeated calls assess within-input stability. Across all three original provenance families, GPT-5.5 identifies the independent update in 10/10 recognition calls per family but over-credits the same-trial report in 5/5 separate aggregation calls per family. Selected Qwen3-4B and Ministral-3-8B conditions show the same cross-task contrast. A study-grouping instruction reduces GPT-5.5 over-credit from 4/5 to 0/5 in each later pure-provenance family. Other model conditions show mixed behavior, and rejected outputs limit several comparisons. The results identify a behavioral gap between elicited lineage recognition and evidence aggregation. The code and data artifacts are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/study-lineage-review-artifact.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 5 figures, 17 tables; 8 pages of main content, with references and appendices thereafter</p></details> |
| **[The Cognitive Cost of LLM Writing: A Reading-Time Comparison with Human Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23715v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Although linguistic differences between human-written and Large Language Model (LLM)-generated texts have been extensively studied, their implications for the cognitive effort involved in reading remain less explored. In psycholinguistics, reading time is commonly used as an indirect measure of cognitive processing effort. To this end, we introduce RoBERTa-RT, a model trained on human eye-tracking data to predict word-level reading times. We evaluate the model on both internal and external test sets, construct a paired dataset of 496 human-written articles and their LLM-generated rewrites, and apply RoBERTa-RT to compare their predicted reading times. Our findings show that LLM-generated rewrites tend to be shorter and have slightly lower predicted total reading times, while exhibiting higher predicted reading times per word. These differences are associated with systematic linguistic changes, including longer and less frequent words, greater lexical diversity, and higher contextual predictability. From a cognitive perspective, our findings suggest that LLM-generated writing may redistribute reading demands: reducing the amount of text to process while increasing the predicted processing effort associated with individual words. This highlights the importance of considering not only the linguistic characteristics of machine-generated content, but also its potential implications for human cognitive processing.</p></details> |  |
| **[VISTA: Steering LLMs with Latent User Values Inferred from a Few Survey Answers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23702v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Personalizing a language model requires knowing what a person values, which stays largely consistent across tasks. | **[IndicDetect: Evaluating Cross-Lingual LLM-Generated Text Detection for Hindi, Telugu, and Tamil](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29919v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid proliferation of LLMs has further heightened the need to develop dependable AI-generated text detection, especially beyond English.
