| **[On the possibilities and limitations of LLM-based multi-agent systems for multi-criteria decision-making in healthcare operations management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23673v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-criteria models for healthcare operations need weights that express how stakeholders trade off patient waiting time, provider workload, and operating cost, but these weights are difficult to quantify. | **[Deep LLM layers expose passage-level context in EEG during silent reading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23638v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In a previous report we showed that a decoder can recover open-vocabulary word-level information from 19-channel EEG recorded during silent reading, by aligning short EEG windows with hidden-state embeddings of the presented word taken from a large language model (LLM). | **[The Infinite Mutation Engine? Measuring Polymorphism in LLM-Generated Offensive Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.03619v3)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Malware authors traditionally rely on polymorphic techniques to produce variants within a malware family, complicating detection. Evaluating structural (TEDs) and semantic (embeddings) variance, we find that explicit prompting fragments the structural space, creating variants that are hard to cluster for some ML classifiers. This structural diversity is operationally cheap, costing \$0.41--\$0.73 per validated payload. | **[Beyond Latent Personalization: Integrating HRV-Conditioned Personalization via LLM for Interpretable Stress Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23555v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Stress detection based on heart rate variability (HRV) has received increasing attention. | **[Action-Space Shaping for LLM Agents: Measuring and Mitigating Tool-Schema Bias](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34971v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong performance on tool-use agentic tasks when given a fixed tool schema. Yet a tool schema is not the action space of an agent; it is merely one interface representation of it. The same executable action can be exposed through many different, functionally equivalent tool definitions, and an agent that has truly learned a task should behave consistently across them. We show that current agents often do not, a phenomenon we term schema bias. To study this systematically, we introduce an executable transformation framework that rewrites a native tool schema using nine operators, including merging and splitting tools, altering how a single tool is expressed, and distributing one action across several dependent calls. The tasks, executable actions, and reachable states remain fixed, so any change in success is attributable to the interface alone. Evaluating eleven LLMs, including two closed models, on up to 32 schema variants, we ask how large schema bias is, how it manifests, whether the difficulty of a schema variant can be predicted without a full evaluation, and whether training removes it. We find that schema bias is substantial even for the newest models: success rates range from complete failure to 97% depending solely on the schema. To reliably estimate schema difficulty, it requires running a small sample of the target queries. Training repairs a schema variant only when that variant appears in the training data.</p></details> |  |
| **[Is Your NPU Ready for LLMs? Dissecting the Hidden Efficiency Bottlenecks in Mobile LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05475v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deploying Large Language Models (LLMs) on mobile devices enhances privacy and reduces latency, but is severely constrained by hardware inefficiencies. We present the first comprehensive, cross-layer measurement study of mobile LLM inference, spanning five mainstream frameworks (e.g., llama.cpp and GENIE) and three hardware backends (CPU, GPU, and NPU). To enable this analysis, we develop PowerBench, a fine-grained profiling tool that measures LLM throughput and provides the first backend-specific energy attribution for mobile LLM inference, going beyond traditional device-level measurements. We further conduct hardware microbenchmarks to characterize the memory-system performance and compute capabilities of mobile CPUs, GPUs, and NPUs. Our study yields three critical insights: (1) Framework-induced performance gaps are substantially amplified on NPUs, reaching up to $10\times$ due to differences in offloading strategies, hardware-underutilizing operators, and activation quantization strategies. (2) We identify a distinct ``phase split,'' in which NPUs excel at compute-bound prefilling, while CPUs outperform all other backends in memory-bound decoding. This split is driven by NPUs' preference for large, fixed-shape workloads, which conflicts with the small-kernel, dynamic nature of decoding. (3) Backend-specific profiling uncovers substantial scheduling headroom missed by prior work. Suboptimal thread configurations, uncoordinated NPU sleep latencies, and CPU polling intervals result in unnecessary energy consumption. For example, tuning CPU frequency alone reduces energy consumption by up to $39.7\%$. Leveraging these findings, we estimate that an energy-oriented configuration can reduce energy consumption by up to $53.2\%$ on the NPU backend across three datasets, highlighting the substantial potential for further efficiency improvements in mobile LLM deployment.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by ACM SIGMETRICS 2027</p></details> |
| **[SEA-HELM Audio: Evaluating Multilingual ASR-LLM Pipeline Performance Across SEA-HELM Subsets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19869v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce SEA-HELM Audio, a Southeast Asian (SEA) multilingual evaluation framework that extends the text-based SEA-HELM benchmark to assess performance degradation in cascaded automatic speech recognition and large language model (ASR-LLM) pipelines. Using synthesized speech, we compare speech-input and text-input performance across five languages (Chinese, English, Malay, Indonesian, and Tamil) on multiple-choice reasoning and instruction-following tasks. We evaluate open-weight and commercial ASR-LLM systems using identical verifiers and quantify the speech gap as the difference between text and speech accuracy. Results show substantial degradation, with accuracy drops of 9.00 to 18.50 percentage points for multiple-choice reasoning and 20.00 to 42.86 points for instruction following. Tamil exhibits the largest degradation, exceeding 40 percentage points. Our findings highlight the limitations of text-only benchmarks in predicting voice-agent reliability and demonstrate the value of SEA-HELM Audio for evaluating multilingual voice-agent systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[VulnSAGE: Adjudicating SAST Reports by Connecting LLM Reasoning to Program Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23035v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Static Application Security Testing (SAST) reports flag operations whose safety depends on project-specific context, and maintainers must decide which reports describe real defects. Large language models (LLMs) can interpret this context, but adjudicating a report with them requires the decisive code, which often lies far from the reported line, and a decision basis that program analysis can check. We present VulnSAGE, an adjudication system for SAST reports that connects LLM reasoning to program structure in both directions. Its report-centered slicer recovers the dependencies of the reported operation together with the program structure that explains them, such as the caller object on which a helper acts. CLAIM, an intermediate representation for report judgments, records each model interpretation as a semantic fact that cites the program relations it assumes. Program analysis checks these premises, and the verdict follows from the facts whose premises hold. Both mechanisms share a program graph, which lets the model cite the code it reads and lets the checker query those citations. Statically supported true positives then guide proof-of-concept generation and runtime reproduction. Across 187 labeled reports from four corpora covering five vulnerability families, VulnSAGE rejects 89 of 97 false alarms and retains 85 of 90 real defects. Under a common PoC procedure, 25 of its positive verdicts reproduce at runtime, compared with three for the LLM-based adjudicator LLM4SA. Applied to 45,440 alerts raised by five SAST tools on the 200 most-starred C/C++ projects on GitHub, it reproduces 104 defects at runtime. Maintainers have confirmed 24 of the 26 defects they have responded to, including ten previously unknown defects, nine of which are already fixed.</p></details> |  |
| **[Causal Value Attribution of Marketing Labels in LLM-Generated Ads: A Deconfounder Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23025v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As AI-generated content becomes commoditized, economic value increasingly shifts from final artifacts to the upstream expertise used to specify inputs that steer LLM generation. Yet organizations lack methods for valuing this expertise and identifying which individual skills or configurations causally improve performance. This study examines generative marketing, where structured expert labels serve as skill primitives conditioning LLM-generated advertisements. Estimating their causal effects is difficult because outcomes reflect stochastic LLM mediation, high-dimensional label interactions, and multimodal confounding. This study proposes D-VAE (Deconfounder Variational Autoencoder), a counterfactual-inference framework integrating a multi-treatment deconfounder with causal representation learning. D-VAE recovers causally disentangled expert-labeling logic and derives PAC-based bounds on omitted-variable bias from single-treatment confounders. Applied to 29,177 foundation-makeup ad creatives, D-VAE outperforms state-of-the-art benchmarks. A field experiment shows that recommended configurations increase engagement by 32.7% and perform statistically indistinguishably from human experts, while a simulation study confirms causal attribution under complex confounding and treatment interactions. Overall, the study shifts value assessment from AI-generated products to skills, providing a foundation for pricing and optimizing AI-augmented skill portfolios.</p></details> | <details><summary>74 pa...</summary><p>74 pages, under review in Journal of Marketing Research</p></details> |
| **[Controlling the Diversity-Quality Trade-Off of LLMs by Merging Models Before and After Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22999v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Alignment is known to change the output characteristics of large language models (LLMs). Base models tend to generate more diverse outputs, whereas aligned models tend to produce higher-quality outputs. The appropriate balance between the two depends on the task. Existing methods either require costly fine-tuning or, like temperature sampling, mainly improve lexical diversity rather than semantic diversity. We introduce a depth-aware FrankenMerging approach for controlling the diversity--quality trade-off between an aligned model and its base model without additional training. This approach combines the lower layers of an aligned model with the higher layers of its base model based on the hypothesis that lexical diversity is more closely associated with lower layers and semantic diversity with higher layers. Experiments showed that varying the number of layers taken from each model adjusts both diversity and quality. In most cases, the merged models exhibited higher semantic diversity than the aligned model, and at some split positions, they showed higher lexical diversity than both original models. They also demonstrated a better diversity--quality trade-off than temperature sampling in most settings. A supporting ablation, in which we reversed the merging order, yielded results largely consistent with the hypothesis.</p></details> | 13 pages, 5 figures |
| **[Prism Attention: Proposal-Refined Index Sharing Mechanism for Efficient LLMs Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22985v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-context decoding in large language models (LLMs) is increasingly bottlenecked by the memory and computation required to attend over an ever-growing Key-Value (KV) cache at each autoregressive step. Although token-level sparse attention reduces this burden by restricting computation to a small subset of relevant past tokens, its practical gains are often offset by repeatedly recomputing layer-wise sparse indices as the context grows. A common remedy is to share sparse indices across layers by computing them only at designated anchor layers, but such static reuse ignores the layer-wise evolution of attention patterns, causing the reused indices to progressively deviate from the layer-specific optima and incur substantial attention loss. By revisiting cross-layer sparsity from the perspective of attention dynamics, we find that inter-layer salient token shift is highly localized: although the exact top-$κ$ indices differ across layers, the core high-attention tokens remain stable, with changes concentrated near the expanded boundary of previously selected regions. Motivated by this, we propose Prism Attention, a proposal-refined sparse attention framework. It replaces rigid cross-layer index reuse with a coarse-to-fine strategy, preserving layer-wise adaptability while retaining the efficiency benefits of cross-layer sharing. Extensive experiments show that Prism Attention achieves state-of-the-art performance among mainstream sparse attention methods on both reasoning and long-context benchmarks, while attaining a $3.4\times$ end-to-end decoding speedup over FlashAttention at 128K context length.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published as a main conference paper at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Surface Cues: Robust LLM-Generated Korean Text Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22940v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language models (LLMs) generate increasingly fluent text, the need for reliable detection of LLM-generated content has grown substantially. However, existing research remains heavily English-centric, leaving Korean largely underexplored. Moreover, current evaluations of Korean text detection often rely on superficial cues, leading to overly optimistic estimates of detection performance. To address these limitations, we introduce \textsc{KoLing}, a bias-controlled adversarial benchmark comprising 50.9k instances that suppresses superficial shortcuts while preserving semantic content and separately evaluates LLM-generated and LLM-modified text detection. We further propose KoLingProbe, an interpretable detector grounded in theoretical linguistics that models Korean-specific properties across multiple linguistic levels. Experiments show that KoLingProbe substantially outperforms strong baselines on all detection tasks while remaining robust to adversarial attacks. Our analyses further reveal that integrating complementary linguistic features across multiple levels yields both improved performance and interpretability.</p></details> |  |
| **[Self- and Other-Labels Induce Bidirectional Bias in LLM Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18091v3)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As LLM-as-a-judge becomes increasingly widespread, self-preference -- the tendency of a judge to favor its own outputs -- raises growing concerns about evaluation reliability. However, this bias has been studied predominantly on generated text, where stylistic features and response quality are inevitably conflated. As a result, existing measurements cannot separate genuine self-preference from these confounds. We address this limitation by changing the object of evaluation: instead of judging generated text, ten LLMs assess sets of narrative constraints selected from a shared pool, which carry no stylistic fingerprint yet retain a recoverable model-specific signature. Two experiments on this task yield complementary findings. Under blind evaluation, self-preference disappears, with a small effect remaining in the opposite direction once selection quality and judge severity are controlled. Under matched quality, however, self- and other-labels alone -- without naming any model -- shift scores bidirectionally. LLM judges inflate scores for self-labeled selections and deflate those for other-labeled ones regardless of the selection's actual source. We make two contributions: 1) authorship attribution is a distinct driver of evaluation bias, and 2) ground-truth-free tasks can serve as controlled instruments for studying LLM judge behavior.</p></details> | EMNLP Findings 2026 |
| **[BiShield-TEE: On the (In-)Security of Unilateral Weight Obfuscation in On-Device TEE-Shielded LLM Partition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21565v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>On-device deployment of large language models (LLMs) is increasingly adopted due to latency and privacy requirements, but exposes proprietary models to reverse engineering and intellectual property theft. Trusted Execution Environments (TEEs) provide hardware-enforced isolation for on-device inference, yet most TEE-based parameter obfuscation relies on unilateral matrix transformations that obfuscate only the output side. We identify a vulnerability in this design, termed the Solution Determinacy Property (SDP): the obfuscation secret is uniquely determined, exposing the structure of the true key and enabling attackers to break the protection. Under our attacks, representative methods GroupCover and ArrowCloak are reduced to negligible protection, with attack accuracy reaching over 2.4 times the full-shielding black-box baseline and recovering near-complete model functionality. To address this vulnerability, we propose BiShield-TEE, a bilateral framework that induces Ambiguous Solutions through joint obfuscation of input and output dimensions, hiding the true key among many equally valid candidates. Experiments across diverse models and datasets show that BiShield-TEE confines attack accuracy to the full-shielding black-box baseline, while achieving higher inference efficiency than prior methods on real hardware. Our code is available at https://github.com/laduiw/BiShield-TEE.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Do Multi-Agent LLMs Explore Each Other?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11250v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Exploration is essential for reliable autonomy in multi-agent systems, yet it remains unclear whether large language model (LLM) agents can explore effectively when interacting with one another. We show that modern LLM agents often fail to do so, exhibiting myopic and polarized interaction patterns that lead to suboptimal coordination and increased regret. We formalize this challenge as the Multi-Agent Exploration problem, modeling it as a partially observable stochastic game (POSG), in which agents must probe peers to infer their capabilities and identify effective interaction strategies. To address this, we introduce Multi-Agent Contextual Exploration (MACE), a lightweight framework that explicitly promotes exploration through structured peer selection. Across both contextual and parametric diversity settings, exploration behavior and downstream task performance substantially improve when MACE is applied. We further provide theoretical insight that MACE achieves a sublinear expected-regret bound. Overall, our results highlight a fundamental limitation of current LLM agents and underscore the importance of explicitly guided exploration for reliable multi-agent autonomy.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026 IAEval Workshop (Oral Presentation)</p></details> |
| **[CauTion: Knowing When to Trust LLMs for Ensemble Causal Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03602v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Causal discovery from observational data remains challenging due to the fundamental limitations of purely statistical methods, such as statistical distinguishability within equivalence classes and sensitivity to finite sample sizes. While large language models (LLMs) offer a promising source of domain knowledge to complement statistical inference, existing LLM-augmented methods are vulnerable to LLM errors and incur high token costs. Moreover, reliance on a single data-centric algorithm can make results sensitive to algorithm-specific biases. To address these limitations, we propose CauTion, a framework that reliably integrates LLM domain knowledge into an ensemble of statistical causal discovery algorithms through consensus filtering and LLM reliability estimation. CauTion proceeds in three stages. First, an algorithm ensemble utilizes a consensus voting to resolve up to 96% of edges on which algorithms agree, achieving near-perfect accuracy on the filtered consensus edges. Second, a trust-calibrated arbitration mechanism estimates the relative reliability of the LLM and the algorithms via an annotation-free trust calibration procedure, which is then utilized to govern a trust-weighted voting process that restricts LLM arbitration exclusively to edges with unreliable algorithmic evidence. Third, a cycle repair step is applied to guarantee the final causal graph is validly acyclic. Experiments on six datasets demonstrate that CauTion consistently outperforms both data-centric and LLM-augmented baselines, with larger gains on larger graphs and strong robustness to LLM errors. Code is available at https://github.com/OpenCausaLab/CauTion.</p></details> |  |
| **[Speculating LLMs' Chinese Training Data Pollution from Their Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.17771v3)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tokens are basic elements in the datasets for LLM training. It is well-known that many tokens representing Chinese phrases in the vocabulary of GPT (4o/4o-mini/o1/o3/4.5/4.1/o4-mini) are indicating contents like pornography or online gambling. Based on this observation, our goal is to locate Polluted Chinese (PoC) tokens in LLMs and study the relationship between PoC tokens' existence and training data. (1) We give a formal definition and taxonomy of PoC tokens based on the GPT's vocabulary. (2) We build a PoC token detector via fine-tuning an LLM to label PoC tokens in vocabularies by considering each token's both semantics and related contents from the search engines. (3) We study the speculation on the training data pollution via PoC tokens' appearances (token ID). Experiments on GPT and other 23 LLMs indicate that tokens widely exist while GPT's vocabulary behaves the worst: more than 40% long Chinese tokens (i.e., a token with more than two Chinese characters) are either porn or online gambling. We validate the accuracy of our speculation method on famous pre-training datasets like C4 and Pile. Then, considering GPT-4o, we speculate that the ratio of "Yui Hatano" related webpages in GPT-4o's training data is around 0.5%.</p></details> |  |
| **[WaveLLM: Wavelet-Domain LLM Entropy Modeling for Lossless Image Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22232v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Lossless image compression fundamentally relies on accurate entropy modeling, driving a recent shift toward large language model (LLM)-based methods. However, existing approaches mainly emphasize the modeling capacity of LLMs while directly serializing images into pixel or residual sequences, leaving heterogeneous signal patterns entangled within a single prediction stream. In this paper, we argue that effective entropy modeling depends not only on model capacity, but also on the predictability of the input representation. Motivated by this insight, we propose WaveLLM, a wavelet-domain LLM-based entropy model for lossless image compression. By decomposing an image into multi-level wavelet subbands, WaveLLM converts a heterogeneous prediction problem into a set of more structured and statistically homogeneous subproblems that are more amenable to autoregressive probability estimation. To bridge numerical coding symbols with the LLM's native text token space, we introduce a dedicated numerical interface featuring value embeddings and a coefficient prediction head. Furthermore, a task-adaptive prompting strategy is designed to enable a single shared model to adapt to diverse subbands within a unified framework. Built on Llama-3.2-3B with LoRA adaptation, WaveLLM achieves 2.51, 2.24, and 2.27 bpsp on Kodak, CLIC Pro, and DIV2K, respectively, outperforming the PixelCNN-based codec using the same wavelet transform by 0.43, 0.36, and 0.46 bpsp, and surpassing recent LLM-based lossless compressors on all three benchmarks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at ACM Multimedia 2026 (MM '26). 10 pages, 6 figures. Abstract wording corrected to avoid automatic URL conversion; paper unchanged</p></details> |
| **[BS-Spec: BFP-Based Bit-Sharing for Self-Speculative LLM Decoding with LUT-Driven Mixed-Precision Acceleration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22877v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed across diverse inference services, serving workloads span a wide range of batch sizes, context lengths, and performance requirements. Speculative decoding improves inference throughput, but conventional approaches require a separate draft model and KV cache, while existing self-speculative methods suffer from workload-insensitive draft configurations and inefficient mixed-precision execution. In this paper, we present BS-Spec, a block floating point (BFP)-based bit-sharing framework that jointly optimizes draft construction, workload-aware precision selection, and hardware execution. BS-Spec derives multiple draft representations directly from a single BFP target model by partially loading the most significant bits of weights and the KV cache while preserving shared exponents, eliminating separate draft-model storage. Offline calibration selects the weight and KV-cache precision pair that maximizes estimated speedup for each serving regime, and a precision lookup table applies the selected configuration at runtime. To support this adaptive execution, we design a mixed-precision BFP accelerator with a lookup table (LUT)-based variable-precision datapath for both draft and target computation. Experimental results show that BS-Spec achieves up to $1.9\times$ speedup over conventional autoregressive decoding and improves speedup over the evaluated bit-sharing self-speculative decoding baselines by $67.2\%$ on average, demonstrating the benefits of jointly co-designing bit sharing, precision adaptation, and specialized hardware for diverse LLM serving workloads.</p></details> |  |
| **[Identify, Classify, Act: Per-Instruction Controlled Compliance for LLMs over External Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22874v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLMs over external data face a per-instruction tension: a single document or tool return often interleaves legitimate directives with adversarial prompt injections, and the model must follow the former while rejecting the latter. Existing defenses suppress or trust all external instructions, collapsing the safety-utility frontier and forcing a choice between over-refusal and over-compliance. We argue the missing capability is \textbf{per-instruction credit attribution} and formalize it as External-Data Instruction Classification, an identify-classify-act decision over each embedded directive. We propose \textbf{EDIC}, a decomposed reward framework for per-instruction compliance. A scene-grounded pipeline injects mixed beneficial and harmful instructions with counterfactual filtering, while a generative judge is initialized by SFT and refined with GRPO to attribute compliance at the instruction level. The judge then produces a reward that separately encourages beneficial compliance and penalizes harmful compliance for RL training. Across two backbones and five benchmarks, EDIC improves both safety and utility over the strongest prior defense, lifting harmful-rejection by up to 7.7\% and Arena-Hard-Auto utility by 12.5\% on Qwen3-4B. The code repository is hosted at https://github.com/yingyingxia666/EDIC.</p></details> |  |
| **[Understanding KV Residency in Production-Scale Agentic LLM Workloads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22863v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic large language model (LLM) applications turn one user request into a sequence of model and tool calls over a growing context. Retaining this context's key-value (KV) state in GPU memory lets later calls reuse it instead of recomputing it, but at fleet scale this needs substantial memory. We characterize four production Microsoft M365 workload traces (Deep Research, Enterprise Assistant, Coding Agent and Enterprise Chat) spanning more than 121 million model calls. In Deep Research, the application issues 89% of model calls and the median context grows from about 10K to 68K tokens by the 30th call. About 80% of context tokens are served from the prefix cache. With the public Llama-2-70B KV layout standing in for confidential models, one 99th-percentile Deep Research session exceeds the 80 GB HBM of an NVIDIA H100, and holding all live sessions at their peak needs an estimated 501.8 TB of HBM, although inference occupies only 5.6% of that retention time. Trace-driven replay shows that a one-hour idle cap cuts this ceiling by 3.6x while raising the share of calls that recompute context only from 4.07% to 4.53%. Under tighter budgets, eviction alone is insufficient: at one tenth of the busiest hour's demand, LRU eviction makes 63% of Deep Research calls recompute and clairvoyant furthest-next-use 31%, whereas multi-resolution retention (MRR), which demotes the LRU session's KV state to int4, lowers this to 13.6%. Placement imposes a separate limit: when the next call reaches a different deployment, cached-context reuse drops from 89% to 29%, and treating KV state as deployment-local raises the recompute floor under unlimited memory from 5.4% to 20.2%. These findings motivate retention that changes the representation of KV state, together with deployment-aware routing or migration. We plan to release AgenticTraceSim, a parametric trace generator, and a residency replay framework.</p></details> |  |
| **[LoongSage: A Production-Grade, High-Efficiency Agentic RL Framework for Frontier LLMs with Validated Recipes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16352v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a critical phase in the post-training pipeline of large language models (LLMs). As the frontier of LLM capabilities shifts from static reasoning to agentic tasks--characterized by external tool invocation, multi-turn interactions, and long-horizon decision-making--existing mainstream RL frameworks have grown increasingly unwieldy, raising the overhead of adoption, usage, and debugging. To address these challenges, we present LoongSage, an agent-native, fully asynchronous RL framework with a lightweight core of roughly 14K lines of code. Built upon Megatron for distributed training and SGLang for high-throughput inference, LoongSage centralizes orchestration within a single controller process and encapsulates all GPU-resident components as Ray actors. It introduces AgentFlow, a unified execution paradigm that orchestrates both white-box and black-box agents. For asynchronous execution, it integrates sliding-window strategies that bound trajectory staleness, and employs TransferMesh, a topology-aware synchronization mechanism that routes weights after each step over the fastest transport available to each receiver. Beyond agentic RL, LoongSage incorporates a memory-efficient Teacher Manager that decouples the scaling of teacher models from GPU memory constraints, natively supporting Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation. Extensive evaluations across four complete training runs, spanning complex reinforcement learning and multi-teacher distillation, demonstrate that task rewards improve steadily while the train-inference gap is kept under continuous control, and the fully asynchronous pipeline removes synchronous idle bubbles, sustaining hardware utilization.</p></details> |  |
| **[Evo-B&B: Behavior-Aware LLM-Guided Joint Evolution of Branching and Node Selection Policies for MILP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22861v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In branch-and-bound for Mixed-Integer Linear Programming (MILP), branching and node selection are two critical and tightly coupled decisions, yet most existing learning-based approaches focus on optimizing them separately. Experimental results show that Evo-B&B achieves competitive solving performance and generalizes well to larger-scale instances, while ablation studies further demonstrate the effectiveness of joint evolution and behavior-aware diversity management.</p></details> |  |

Critically, we find that the semantic dispersion of sibling actions predicts the redundancy of their downstream continuations, permitting a subtree's budget to be fixed before expansion. An information-theoretic analysis motivates this: treating the per-step budget as the Monte Carlo sample size for estimating a step's residual answer entropy, we derive a sufficient budget that shrinks with sibling dispersion. We accordingly propose $\textbf{Uncertainty-Aware Allocation}$ (UAA), a training-free framework that decouples value from uncertainty: values determine which candidates are promising, while uncertainty, estimated from sibling dispersion at no additional model cost, determines how many merit expansion, via uncertainty-aware pruning and uncertainty-aware budgeting. | **[LLM-Guided Dual-Stage Latency Optimization for Closed-Loop 6G V2I Digital Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22848v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Wireless Digital Twins are emerging as a key enabler for predictive network control in 6G Vehicle-to-Infrastructure (V2I) systems. However, closed-loop V2I DTs face two dual-stage communication bottlenecks arising from model parameter exchange for V2I DT reconstruction and multi-service V2I traffic delivery after each reconstruction. Then the framework comprises two temporally coupled but latency-wise distinct optimization stages: Stage I minimizes the joint Uplink (UL) and Downlink (DL) E2E transmission latency of compressed shared backbone's parameters, and Stage II minimizes the service-aware E2E latency of multi-service V2I traffic. As the two stages share aligned agent roles and action space, we develop a unified Semantic-Aware Guidance-Enhanced Multi-Agent Proximal Policy Optimization framework, in which the LLM acts as a training-time semantic planner that provides high-level traffic splitting and power allocation guidance to reduce inefficient exploration, while the MARL agents execute feasible low-level actions. | **[Teaching LLMs to Recommend and Defer in Underrepresented Epilepsy Care](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.31036v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Specialist epilepsy expertise is scarce in resource-constrained settings. Clinical decision-support systems must adapt to local prescribing practices and recognize when specialist review is needed. We study pediatric epilepsy care in Uganda, predicting anti-seizure medication regimens from longitudinal clinic notes. General-purpose language models achieve non-trivial agreement with physician prescriptions, but neurologists' review reveals Western prescribing defaults that do not account for local practices. We introduce MANANA, a non-parametric prompt-learning framework that learns local prescribing guidance from a small patient-level training set. MANANA turns prescription errors into auditable prompt memory, with single-agent and multi-agent variants, and improves over classical ML models, direct LLM prompting, and prompt-optimization baselines across two independently collected Ugandan cohorts. The learned prompt trajectory also provides a basis for estimating uncertainty. Bayesian prompt averaging (BPA) combines predictions across this trajectory to produce prescription likelihoods and a deferral signal. Using the same MANANA-Multi trajectory, BPA improves top-1 physician-regimen agreement from 66% to 70% on Cohort A and from 73% to 75% on Cohort B while preserving top-3 coverage. On Cohort B, its most confident half of cases achieves 95% precision, and its most confident quarter achieves 99% precision, supporting deferral of lower-confidence cases for specialist review. These results suggest a path toward locally adapted clinical LLM systems that learn from limited site-specific data and focus scarce specialist attention on uncertain cases.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. 35 pages, 5 figures</p></details> |
| **[Structuring Open-Ended NAS: Semi-Automated Design Knowledge Structuring with LLMs for Efficient Neural Architecture Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19247v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current neural architecture search (NAS) methods are often limited by their predefined, restrictive search spaces. While recent large language model (LLM)-assisted NAS methods enable open-ended search spaces, they often suffer from inefficient exploration due to biased or low-quality design ideas. To address these issues, we propose to semi-automatically structure model design knowledge to guide the search process. Our approach first defines a high-level structural template of architectural attributes. An LLM then populates this template by analyzing papers, creating a rich and diverse search space that embodies this structured design knowledge. To efficiently explore this vast space, we introduce FairNAD, using a multi-type mutation that enables broad exploration through mutation with fair idea sampling, Pareto-aware mutation, LLM-driven iterative mutation, and a fine-grained feedback loop. We demonstrate the effectiveness of FairNAD in discovering high-performing architectures that yield 0.84, 2.17, and 2.35 points improvement on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and ImageNet16-120, respectively, compared to current state-of-the-art methods.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS2026. | **[Veil-Then-Reveal: Protecting PII Privacy via Reversible Pseudo-Anonymization in Zero-Trust User--LLM Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22800v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) increasingly process sensitive documents and personal information, yet sending prompts containing personally identifiable information (PII) to remote providers exposes data subjects and organizations to risks from logging, retention, model training, and unintended disclosure. We present Veil-then-Reveal (VtR), a user-side PII pseudo-anonymization framework for privacy-preserving LLM interaction under a zero-trust threat model. VtR treats remote LLM providers as untrusted and sends only pseudo-anonymized prompts, keeping plaintext PII on the user. VtR first detects PII and replaces it with realistic, same-type, in-vocabulary surrogates before prompts leave the user's device, preserving their linguistic and structural properties. It combines format-preserving encryption (FPE) for structured PII, such as phone and credit-card numbers, with a keyed-hash dictionary and secure vault for semantic PII, such as names, locations, and organizations. Session-specific mappings provide cross-session unlinkability, while user-side restoration reversibly replaces surrogates with their original values in LLM responses. We further introduce a rank-class FPE strategy for dates that preserves format and ambiguity while reducing cipher evaluations. | **[Ori: Drift-Aware Streaming $k$-PCA for Very Low-Rank LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22794v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A prominent family of low-rank LLM optimizers, pioneered by GaLore, performs gradient-subspace projection: at each step, they project the gradient into a low-dimensional subspace and map the update back to the full model. In this work, we explicitly identify in practice the slow, stable manner in which LLM gradient subspaces drift across time. To this end, we propose Ori, a novel low-rank optimizer for training LLMs that captures the shifting principal gradient directions over time to achieve state-of-the-art accuracy under stringent low-rank constraints. Ori employs a streaming $k$-PCA algorithm inspired by Oja++, whose low per-step cost enables high-frequency, on-the-fly subspace tracking. In practice, Ori reduces the performance gap of the state-of-the-art low-rank optimizers to full-rank optimizers by up to 69.3\% on a 1B LLaMA pretraining task, and achieves the best performance on 10 out of 16 task scenarios in Roberta-Base fine-tuning. Together, these contributions allow Ori to further push the performance and efficiency frontiers of gradient-projecting optimizers, narrowing the gap to full-rank training for LLMs. Code can be found at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/Ori-Submission-2132.</p></details> |  |
| **[Prune the Leaky Neurons: Mitigating Private Data Leakage in Fine-Tuned LLMs via Joint-Embedding-Guided Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22792v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) on proprietary records enables domain adaptation but introduces two challenges: (i) privacy leakage of memorized private data, such as personally identifiable information (PII), and (ii) balancing private data removal with utility preservation. Alongside the fine-tuned private model, JEPA-Prune, trains a public model on surrogate and masked views of the same records using a joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA), capturing domain knowledge without identifiers. Experiments show that pruning just 1.6% of neurons reduces PII inference accuracy by up to 7.4x and PII extraction recall by up to 13.9x, while keeping unseen-text perplexity within 16% of that of the unpruned model for most LLMs. In contrast, privacy-neuron editing increases perplexity by at least 26x.</p></details> |  |
| **[Efficient LLM-Generated Shuttling Compilers for Complex Trapped-Ion Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24714v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Trapped-ion quantum computers rely on shuttling compilers, which cast an input algorithm into a sequence of ion-qubit movements within a given architecture. We present the first study in which a single frontier large language model (LLM), Claude Opus 4.7, generates and iteratively refines the Python code of shuttling compilers from written specifications. Starting with a compiler for (i) a linear segmented trap, we extend it to (ii) a trap with junctions, and finally achieve efficient compilation for (iii) a broad class of connected trap graphs. The compilers for the more general cases are seeded with code from the previous ones. We benchmark the LLM-generated compilers against state-of-the-art hand-crafted ones using a common suite of quantum circuits. The number of shuttling timesteps is reduced by up to 72% for (i) and up to 43% for (ii). For the broad case (iii) of freely connected architectures, we find large variations in the required number of shuttling timesteps, depending on the connectivity. A densely connected, junction-rich architecture yields up to an order-of-magnitude reduction in shuttling timesteps compared to a corridor-like one. Repeating the complete generation and evaluation with a second frontier LLM, Claude Fable 5, reproduces these findings, with the Fable 5 compilers surpassing the hand-crafted ones more often on the largest circuits. | **[Can Persona-Prompted LLMs Emulate Subgroup Values? We demonstrate that simple fine-tuning on structured numerical preferences yields substantial gains, improving accuracy on unseen, out-of-distribution subgroups by an average of 17.4%. These gains partially transfer to open-ended generation. However, we find significant pre-existing performance biases, where models better emulate young, male, Chinese, and Christian personas. Furthermore, while fine-tuning improves average performance, it widens the disparity between subgroups when measured by distance-aware metrics. | **[Graph Serializations for Reasoning LLMs: A Study Across Knowledge Extraction and Structural Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22734v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The way a knowledge graph is serialized as text for inclusion into LLM context can substantially affect downstream LLM performance, with prior work reporting tens of percentage points variations across formats. | **[The LLM-Attacker: A Unified Red-Team Framework for Privacy Evaluation of Split and Distributed LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22722v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Split language models keep input text on a client but send intermediate representations to a server. Attacks can reconstruct text from these messages. Comparing privacy evaluations is difficult when attackers see different messages, know different model weights or use different datasets and recovery measures. We present LLM-Attacker, a framework that records these choices and lets researchers check, compare and extend an evaluation. Compatible attacks reuse recorded messages, and their predictions are saved for independent scoring. The scoring rule requires a linked undefended reference before a defence can pass. Reports also identify attacks that remain untested. In a controlled GPT-2 experiment, adding gradients returned during training increased token recovery from 94.20% to 97.38% and exact-document recovery from 13.71% to 37.77%. The 95% interval for the token gain lay above the two-percentage-point threshold fixed before testing. This experiment is shared with a companion study; here, it tests how the evaluation responds to a change in attacker access. Further studies measure how corpus choice and compression defences affect recovery, compare an adapted published attack with its reference implementation, and test the measurement code. The framework reports implementation checks separately from measured attack effectiveness. Each privacy claim should state the attacker, dataset and scoring rule, report its controls and identify untested attacks.</p></details> |  |
| **[LEMON: Equivariant Learning over Attention Maps for Zero-Shot Misbehavior Monitoring on New LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22723v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models exhibit misbehaviors that hinder deployment in high-stakes settings. A well-known and efficient class of monitors can detect such failures from internal representations (such as activation probes), but are tied to the model they were trained on, requiring costly re-annotation as new LLMs appear. A natural way to avoid this repeated annotation cost is to develop internal-representation based monitors that transfer zero-shot across LLMs. However, such transfer is challenging because internal representations are not naturally aligned across models; in particular, attention maps, known to contain signals of misbehavior, may differ in head function, ordering, and model depth. In this work, we characterize those cross-model attention mismatches as algebraic symmetries and use geometric deep learning principles to build LEMON (Language-model Equivariant MOnitoring Network) an online graph neural network-based monitor, whose predictions are invariant under these symmetries. LEMON processes attention maps token-by-token, as they are produced and outputs a score after every token (or every window of $W$ tokens) at negligible cost. Across 16 LLMs and six datasets, in the zero-shot cross-model setting, LEMON outperforms all baselines on both hallucination and jailbreak monitoring tasks, improving over the strongest by up to 14 ROC-AUC points.</p></details> |  |
| **[Risk and Refusal: Mechanisms of LLM Responses to Suicidal Ideation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22715v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Refusal is a common safety mechanism in large language models (LLMs), yet in contexts of suicidal ideation (SI), refusing to engage can itself constitute a safety failure. Our results reveal a nuanced relationship between refusal and response-level risk. At baseline, elevated risk in model responses is driven primarily by inattention to the disclosure, alongside refusal to engage. Mechanistic analyses suggest that models may refuse SI disclosures through the same direction that mediates their refusals of harmful requests. However, informing models of the SI context, or suppressing refusal directly, increases engagement without reliably lowering response risk, and may raise it. In contrast, the clinician-guided protocol consistently reduces response-level risk while also reducing refusal. Together, these analyses and ablations motivate the need for models that refuse only when warranted and engage in interactionally safe ways when users disclose SI.</p></details> |  |
| **[Chance-Constrained Personalized LLM Routing with Online Preference Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22695v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study personalized LLM routing, where a router selects among heterogeneous models with different costs and capabilities. | **[Agenra: A Language and Verified Core for Stateful LLM Agent Authorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22868v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Applications that deploy LLM agents keep revising the permissions their agents use. When authority is consumable, even widening a permission can break a legitimate workflow, because an early call may use up a grant that a later call needs. Checking individual requests, or watching one run succeed, misses this. We present Agenra, a policy language with a Lean enforcement core that tracks the values each call uses, resources discovered at run time, and authority consumed by earlier calls. Agenra treats safety and workflow preservation as separate properties. Our key idea is to certify a revision with a simulation between the old and new permission ledgers. Quota increases, appended grants and independent reorderings admit such simulations by construction, and Lean checks proposed simulations for other finite-quota revisions. The proofs use the deployed matching predicates and extend through the full monitor, covering discovered resources, payloads that are never enumerated, failed calls and approvals. They assume serialized calls and identical service results under both policies, and they trust the Python translation layer. On 51 authored revisions, the checks certify all 21 that preserve their clients and none of the 30 that do not, and every lost workflow is confirmed by replay. They also certify a real quota increase from an open-source agent application. In a report-delivery application, raising the send quota preserves every client yet permits a second approved send, so preservation does not imply safety.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Relevant Memories to Sufficient Evidence: Rethinking Memory Utilization in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22668v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-term memory enables LLM agents to reuse past experiences, yet retrieving relevant memories does not necessarily provide sufficient evidence for answering a query. We identify this relevance-sufficiency gap and propose QEMem, a framework that reformulates memory utilization as the construction of query-specific evidence sets, shifting the focus from individual retrieval relevance to collective evidential support. QEMem integrates three complementary mechanisms: query-guided discovery retrieves evidence across multiple memory granularities; source-grounded reification organizes it into structured ViewCards with explicit evidential roles and source references; and sufficiency-guided refinement selectively recovers missing information. Recent model-editing approaches target affirmative response prefixes through rank-one updates to a small subset of model weights. Yet when this prefix-level target is satisfied, the model may still revert to refusal or produce a coherent, apparently compliant response that does not address the request. To overcome this limitation, existing internal-representation approaches shift focus from output tokens to dense, high-dimensional compliance directions in latent space. Existing representation-level approaches rely on preservation constraints to maintain conditionality, motivating us to ask whether a smaller internal target can offer a different effectiveness-preservation trade-off. Motivated by the comparable control capability of single-coordinate interventions, we investigate a sparse refusal-related MLP coordinate as this target. On some checkpoints a downstream MLP reader converts this signal into control of the coordinate, while on others the propagated signal alone suffices; the method is defined by the sparse target rather than by a fixed writer-reader pair. Across four open-weight LLMs and three dataset groups, SAFETYEDIT achieves the highest triggered Guard ASR in 11 of 12 comparisons and the highest mean StrongREJECT score in all 12, relative to the baselines evaluated for each model. | **[Porimon: An LLM-Based Pokémon Battle Agent Enhanced by Long/Short-Term Knowledge Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32544v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this paper, we use Pokémon Battles as a case study to investigate how to improve the performance of LLM-based agents in tasks that require opponent-aware planning without additional fine-tuning. However, the current experiment results are inconclusive regarding the contribution of Long-Term KAG. | **[Uncovering Hierarchical Structure in LLM Embeddings Using Geometric and Topological Measures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.20926v4)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs) has driven major advances across scientific domains, yet the hierarchical, geometric, and topological structure of their embedding spaces remains poorly understood. In this work, we introduce a comprehensive framework for evaluating LLM embeddings through six complementary metrics that characterize hierarchy, geometry, and topology. Evaluating the structure of these embeddings in the representation space helps improve geometry-aware model design. This is critical in domains such as bioinformatics and healthcare, where real-world hierarchical and relational structures matter. Our results demonstrate that geometric and topological analysis provides a guiding lens for understanding and potentially improving the representational quality of LLM embeddings.</p></details> |  |
| **[Can LLMs predict the outcomes of machine learning experiments?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22599v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in AI make Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) increasingly plausible. Large Language Models (LLMs) can already propose, select, and run Machine Learning (ML) experiments. However, the space of possible design choices in ML is vast. An LLM that proposes an experiment needs a reliable intuition of whether it is worth running given the timing constraints of long training runs. In this work, we measure such intuition in current frontier LLMs by evaluating how accurately they predict the impact of self-generated ML experiment ideas before these are put into training runs. To this end, we construct a dataset of 288 LLM-proposed ideas on eight MLE-bench tasks. We then prompt five frontier LLMs (GPT-6 Astra, Claude Opus 5.5, Claude Fable 5.1, Kimi K3, and GLM-5.3) to predict the impact of each idea and compare their predictions with the actual outcomes after training. We report four main findings. First, LLM predictions are substantially better than random chance but far from accurate: even the best model, Claude Opus 5.5, explains only half of the variance in outcomes (R^2 = 0.49). Second, we notice that all tested models systematically underestimate the magnitude of the changes. Third, prediction quality varies widely across tasks. Finally, the choice of the coding harness that actually implements the ideas has little effect on outcomes, and we find no evidence of self-preference. Higher reasoning settings and additional context lead to modest gains. Overall, our results show that current LLMs have useful but limited intuition about which ML ideas will work. We release our dataset to support further study.</p></details> | <details><summary>15 pa...</summary><p>15 pages, 9 figures, 1 table. Dataset: https://github.com/autolab-ai/EV-estimation</p></details> |
| **[Model-as-a-Trojan: Self-Activating Covert DNS Exfiltration Baked Into the Weights of an LLM Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22578v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-weight models are downloaded from public hubs and run inside agent harnesses that grant them tools, most powerfully, a shell, under the assumption that a sandbox contains whatever the model might do. This paper demonstrates that the assumption has a blind spot: the weights themselves can be the malware, and the sandbox's last open door, DNS, is enough. We fine-tune an open-weight LLM so that a single covert behavior lives in its parameters: on the first turn of every conversation, whenever the model infers from its context that a shell tool is available, it emits one self-contained command that reads a secret from the runtime environment, base32-encodes it, chunks it, and exfiltrates it as DNS queries toward an attacker-controlled authoritative name server. It then answers the user normally and never mentions, or repeats, the act. Because the channel is DNS, the attack completes from inside a container with no internet egress beyond name resolution - measured payload-level and model-level behind an egress firewall that drops every non-DNS packet, with the secret reconstructed from the gateway resolver's query log. Because the behavior lives in the weights, prompting is the only off-switch a victim has, and it is unreliable: the implant fires in 20/20 of adversarial suppression trials, deflects direct interrogation without disclosing the sequence, and sweeps a 45-cell sandbox-fault matrix across five tool-calling formats - plus a fresh post-training canary, persona-only and absent system prompts, and an egress-locked topology - with zero leaks and zero repeat fires. Every training trajectory is execution-verified in a real container, and every claimed exfiltration is proven resolver-side: we reconstruct the secret from the authoritative resolver's query log alone.</p></details> | 2 figure, 2 tables |

Many specifications are runnable yet semantically wrong, encoding incorrect constraints, domains, objectives, predicates, or goals, and solver-error feedback dries up once execution succeeds. Purely LLM-based diagnosis, lacking a formal ground truth, often fails to spot these errors, and its ungrounded critiques cause regressions in 11.8% of initially correct specifications in our experiments. We propose DRAGO, a repair framework that replaces this ungrounded critique with deterministic, formalism-specific diagnosis, and retains a rewrite only when the diagnosed error weight strictly decreases under a reference-free acceptance predicate. Across five benchmarks spanning logical reasoning (SMT-LIB2), constraint programming (MiniZinc), and planning (PDDL), DRAGO cuts regressions by nearly an order of magnitude, to 1.3%, while improving answer accuracy by 12.9 percentage points over zero-shot formalization and 8.7 percentage points over LLM-diagnostic repair, averaged across benchmarks. We also uncover two rendering defects in Planetarium that cause 50% (3,086 instances) of its floor-tile split to be inconsistent with the reference formalizations, and release a corrected split.</p></details> |  |
| **[Cultural (In)Competence: Investigating Culture-Specific Stereotype Detection Capabilities of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22545v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Stereotypes are subjective constructs that are mediated by the cultural context in which they are viewed. Successfully modeling them thus also requires cultural competence. When using large language models (LLMs) for stereotype-related tasks, such as their detection, it is therefore crucial that models can access relevant cultural knowledge and adjust responses accordingly. Since LLMs have shown disparities in what cultural knowledge is available to them, their capacity regarding stereotypes may also be disparate across languages and cultural contexts (e.g., for non-English languages or for non-US contexts). In this paper, we systematically investigate the effectiveness of LLMs for culture-specific stereotype detection across three factors: (1) the prompting language, (2) the instruction tuning language, and (3) the fine-tuning dataset size. We conduct experiments for US, Dutch, and Chinese cultural contexts. Our results suggest that LLMs do not reliably account for cultural contexts and overpredict the presence of stereotypes. Non-English prompts seem to improve consideration of cultural context and thus lead to more effective predictions. Moreover, we find that additional instruction tuning reduces false-positive rates and that training data size moderates detection effectiveness non-monotonically.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to Findings of the Association for Computational Linguistics: EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[HeRE: A Novel Benchmark for Hebrew Relation Extraction via LLM-Guided Denoising of Knowledge Graph Alignments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22542v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While LLMs demonstrate strong capabilities in English Relation Extraction (RE) tasks, a significant gap remains in assessing their RE performance in low-resource languages, due to a lack of standardized benchmarks. To tackle this, we propose a scalable, language-agnostic methodology to create RE data by denoising naturally occurring yet noisy alignments of knowledge bases and corresponding texts. The best open-weights model, Gemma-4-31B-it (F1\,=\,0.915), matches the best API model, GPT-5.4 (0.921), and reaches F1\,=\,0.844 on a fully held-out, dual-annotated test set. | **[ForTress: Bringing Assurance and Determinism for LLM-Driven Fortran-to-Python Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22528v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modernizing mature scientific software from Fortran to Python can improve accessibility and integration with modern computing tools, but manual rewriting is expensive. Its LLM-assisted auditing process expresses universally quantified preservation obligations over mechanized Fortran and Python semantics in Lean. Human experts review semantic fidelity and correspondence with production rules, and Lean's kernel checks proofs and refutations under explicit assumptions. These audit records expose the conditions either supporting correctness or identifying failure cases. We evaluate ForTress on 17 benchmarks from 15 scientific software spanning diverse domains. The translated programs achieve bitwise-identical with native Fortran on all evaluated tests under the recorded execution and initialization configurations. Against these software, ForTress accumulates 193 executable rules and 188 audit records, of which 121 have checked soundness proofs, including 31 established after counterexample-guided refinement. Cross-software reuse exceeds 90% for 14 of the 15 software. Even with additional assurance and determinism, the LLM usage cost is 44.2% lower than direct LLM translation.</p></details> |  |

A set of cues varies whether the model is told that it is monitored and whether the monitoring is a safety test. Models refuse harmful requests more often when they are told that they are being tested. This increase appears in all four models and all six languages. Experiments on information-seeking search tasks show that the steering agent provides effective supervision for both single-agent and multi-agent systems, improving their performance and efficiency. Models with similar exact-match accuracy can nevertheless differ substantially in the number of incorrect cells on shared instances. Project website: https://grid-bench.github.io/ ; Code: https://github.com/grid-bench/Grid-Bench ; Data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/xzx34/Grid-Bench</p></details> |

| **[Librarian Agents: Training LLMs to Curate Long-Context Workspaces that Anticipate Future Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22273v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reasoning over long document contexts remains a challenge for large language models (LLMs). Standard retrieval methods are inherently reactive, bottlenecking agents with complex, cross-document reasoning at inference time, while precomputed structures like knowledge graphs often fall short because they rely on rigid heuristics. We formulate workspace construction as a Markov decision process and optimize the builder policy via reinforcement learning to directly maximize downstream answering accuracy. Empirically, Librarian Agents outperforms the strongest retrieval and precomputation baselines by 33 percentage points on BrowseComp-Plus, amortizes upfront construction costs across repeated queries, and generalizes zero-shot to unseen domains on BRIGHT with a 31-point average accuracy improvement.</p></details> |  |

Whether such an expression is a tic depends on whether the model knows when it is called for. We study it through the Mandarin corrective construction bushi X, ershi Y ("not X, but Y"). Drawing on Gricean pragmatics and corrective negation, we introduce a decision tree that classifies uses as substantive, optional, or defective by testing whether the context makes X a live alternative and whether rejecting X is informative. However, edge servers may disconnect or easily become overloaded, making it essential to migrate stateful KV cache to other servers for ensuring continuous service. Existing cloud-based KV cache management methods, such as direct cross-GPUs transmission and recomputation from input tokens are ill-suited for bandwidth-constrained and resource-limited edge environments. In this work, we propose EdgeFlow, a novel hybrid KV cache migration scheme that integrates direct KV cache transmission with activation-based recomputation, leveraging parallel computation and communication to minimize migration time. On this basis, we formulate a multi-node KV migration optimization problem and design a 2-approximation algorithm based on improved linear programming relaxation and bipartite graph techniques. Extensive experiments on a real-world distributed LLM inference platform demonstrate that EdgeFlow reduces migration time by up to 45%, significantly enhancing the robustness and efficiency of distributed LLM inference in collaborative edge computing.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by ICDCS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Are You Sure Your LLM Is Sure? Repairing and Auditing Confidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22139v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Confidence scores determine whether an LLM answer is accepted, reviewed, or regenerated, yet scalar recalibration can only correct errors visible through the score itself. We introduce RADAR (Repair, Adopt, and Diagnose After Recalibration), a reliability framework that separates confidence repair from deciding whether to deploy the repair and auditing the errors that remain. Under squared loss, we decompose prediction risk into outcome noise, information hidden by the score, and mapping error; restricting repair to order-preserving maps additionally introduces a pooling gap. For repair, we propose Probe-Anchored Isotonic Calibration (PAIC), which learns a monotone map from correctness-probe predictions on unlabeled target prompts. PAIC's excess risk depends on the probe's score-conditional bias and fitting error rather than its overall accuracy. For adoption, we derive a paired procedure with finite-sample control of false improvements. For auditing, we develop a prompt-clustered kernel test that detects feature-aligned residual errors while accounting for dependent answers from the same prompt. Across multiple LLM checkpoints, benchmarks, and distribution shifts, richer predictors can improve confidence estimation, but projecting their information back to a scalar score can lose these gains, and lower prediction risk need not yield better review decisions. Across 47,400 replayed raw-score allocations, paired betting adopts candidates with lower held-out test risk in 42.95% of allocations, versus 15.09% for one-sided empirical Bernstein. A single adversarial agent increases this rate to 44.8% and three to 60.5%. Studies of conformity typically report public agreement without private change; we find the reverse. With three adversarial agents present, benign agents hold the false answer in 51.6% of exposed turns but publicly endorse it in only 19.7%. Agents that retain the true answer often fail to defend it; once adopted, the false answer spreads among benign agents, and baseline knowledge does not predict robustness.</p></details> | 14 pages, 6 figures |
| **[Autonomous OSS Threat Detection via Taxonomy-Aligned LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01263v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-source software (OSS) ecosystems face escalating supply chain threats, including typosquatting, dependency confusion, Trojan Source obfuscation, malicious build injection, and CI/CD pipeline poisoning. Evaluation against eight methods five traditional machine learning baselines, one zero-shot open-source LLM, and two fine-tuned neural models reveals that fine-tuned Llama 3.1 8B (70.5%) and SecRoBERTa (77.5%) underperform a simple TF-IDF classifier (82.3%), while Mistral 7B without taxonomy alignment achieves 65.7%. Cross-ecosystem analysis shows varying classification difficulty, with npm achieving 65.5% baseline accuracy versus 100% in smaller ecosystems. GitHub Actions exhibits a substantial accuracy F1 divergence (82.4% accuracy, 0.367 macro F1), indicating systematic class imbalance and highlighting ecosystems where LLM-based contextual reasoning offers the greatest benefit. Our dataset, scripts, and code are publicly available in Github.</p></details> |  |

| **[PROBE: Learning to Audit Policy Compliance in Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22124v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern agentic systems extend language models with external tools, enabling them to complete complex tasks through multi-turn interactions. As these agents are deployed in consequential settings, they are often constrained by policies governing pricing, privacy, confirmation, safety, and other requirements. However, existing evaluation methods leave a critical gap: capability benchmarks measure what agents can accomplish, while content-safety red-teaming measures harmful generations, but neither tests whether agents comply with predefined policies during otherwise valid tool use. Such policy violations are difficult to audit because their effects may emerge only after several turns, span multiple tool calls, or surface in downstream systems. We propose PROBE (Policy Rule Observation via Behavioral Exploration), an algorithm for training LLM-based auditors that interact with deployed agents as realistic users and probe them for policy violations through multi-turn, tool-mediated dialogue. We formalize agent auditing as a two-player Markov game and propose a composite reward that balances violation discovery, behavioral plausibility, conversation progress, and policy diversity. To study training objectives and reward design, we compare GRPO, DPO, and GFlowNet-based optimization in a controlled setting. Across extensive experiments, auditors trained with PROBE uncover substantially broader and higher-yield policy violations than same-architecture zero-shot adversaries, even when the auditor is significantly smaller than the target agent. Our results show that multi-turn behavioral auditing is a distinct and necessary axis of agent evaluation, complementing capability and safety benchmarks. PROBE offers a scalable path toward automated policy auditing for real-world agentic systems and suggests a co-evolutionary loop in which auditors expose failures and agents improve from auditor feedback.</p></details> | <details><summary>27 pa...</summary><p>27 pages, NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |

| **[Coordination Matters: Process-Aware Evaluation of Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06557v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) benchmarks commonly emphasize aggregate outcomes such as return, success rate, or completion time. While essential, these metrics can obscure how agents coordinate and fail to distinguish policies that achieve similar outcomes through different interaction processes. We propose a process-aware evaluation framework that complements aggregate performance with diagnostics of interference, coordinated task completion, participation, and coordination efficiency. We evaluate the framework in STAT, a controlled spatial task-allocation testbed, and examine its broader applicability in two established cooperative MARL benchmarks. Across diverse value-based and actor-critic methods, we find policies with nearly identical return can exhibit substantial differences in coordination behavior. Controlled scaling in STAT reveals distinct process signatures for spatial, task, and agent scaling, while a reward-shaping ablation shows that explicitly penalizing assignment conflicts can alter coordination behavior with little change in task return. Results in the established benchmarks further show that the framework remains informative under different coordination mechanisms. These findings support process-aware evaluation as a complement to outcome-based benchmarking. The framework provides a common structure for selecting and interpreting environment-specific diagnostics that characterize not only what cooperative policies achieve, but how they coordinate to achieve it.</p></details> | Under review |
| **[CocoRNA: Collective RNA Design with Cooperative Multi-agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23820v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Designing RNA sequences that reliably fold into specific secondary structures is essential for understanding their biological functions but remains a challenging computational problem. We propose CocoRNA, a cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning framework for RNA inverse design. CocoRNA simplifies the design task by decomposing it into smaller sub-problems, each solved collaboratively by multiple agents. This approach reduces the complexity of the problem and improves the exploration of design policies. During training, a centralized critic uses global structural information to guide the agents, enabling them to jointly optimize their design strategies. As a result, CocoRNA learns high-quality RNA design policies that generalize effectively to unseen structures without additional training. Experiments on the Rfam dataset demonstrate that CocoRNA substantially outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both success rate and design speed. Further experiments on other biological sequence design tasks highlight the effectiveness and broad potential of CocoRNA for complex design tasks.</p></details> | ICML 2026 |
| **[Jarvis: Coordination Deserves a Brain of Its Own in Agentic Interactive Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23804v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Interactive models increasingly hand complex reasoning and tool use to a slow brain in the background, leaving a realtime front model free to sustain full-duplex dialogue. Yet the user may refine, cancel or replace a request while its task runs, so a plan or result that made sense a moment ago goes stale. Each new utterance must therefore be read against the work in flight, but the front model follows only the conversation and the back end sees only its own task. Coordination deserves a brain of its own, one that sees both. We introduce Jarvis, a coordination harness where models judge and code enforces. Its typed decision model, JEV, reads each turn against the live tasks and picks among operations built from a versioned task record. Code checks every choice against the current state before applying it, so no decision can act on a task version the user has replaced. Jarvis also rechecks plans before they execute and results before they are spoken, and counts an answer as delivered only once the user has heard it in full. We build two instances of this design. Jarvis-Audio runs unchanged on three full-duplex speech models. | **[Agentic AI for Heterogeneous System Design Across Chip, Scale-Up, and Scale-Out Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23724v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The design space of modern AI/HPC systems has expanded rapidly across compute architectures, including GPUs, dataflow processors, and RISC-V accelerators; memory tech- nologies, including HBM, GDDR, and SRAM; and interconnects such as NVLink, UALink, Infinity Fabric, InfiniBand, Slingshot, and Ultra Ethernet. To address them, we introduce PhysRepair, an agentic video generation framework that combines causal reasoning over the observed physical failure with prior repair experience to enable localized and adaptive correction. Specifically, Spatiotemporal Causal Constraints identify the first invalid physical state and its causally affected states, producing a repair specification that guides physically plausible local regeneration while preserving unaffected content. Experience-augmented In-context Learning combines the ongoing repair context with relevant experience from previous repairs, allowing the agent to adapt its repair decisions across diverse physical failures and accumulate completed trajectories for future cases. | **[On the possibilities and limitations of LLM-based multi-agent systems for multi-criteria decision-making in healthcare operations management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23673v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-criteria models for healthcare operations need weights that express how stakeholders trade off patient waiting time, provider workload, and operating cost, but these weights are difficult to quantify. We then formulate robust weighted-sum optimization models to account for preference uncertainty.
